PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

MANY a man is expected to be the architect of his son in-law's fortune. THE Deadly Parallel—The railroad track. WHAT is done cannot be undone, especially if it is a bard-boiled egg. "I AM a self-made man." "Well, you need not Waste any money securing a patent." A THEORIST is a man with perfect confidence in bis imagination. WISDOM is the faculty of knowing what delusion one is happiest in believing. DEDBRORE (borrowing 'five) : "Words can never repay this kinduess, old man." Cumso : "No; I don't think they can." SCRIBBLER : "Have you read my new novel, 'A Story Without a Hero'? " Carper: " Yes. I think it's misnamed, however. Any man who reads it through proves himself a hero."

RAILWAY SEr-PERINTENDENT (to carriage inspec- tor) : "Any of the carriages out of order ?'. "Yes ; No. 412 is unfit for service." "Well, use it only for excursions after this." MASTER "How was this vase smashed Mary?" Mary: "If you please, sir, it tumbled down and broke itself." Master: " Ilumph ! The automatic brake again !"

PERFECTLY SERENE.—" Ever quarrel with your wife?" "No." "Have any trouble with your servants ?" "No." "Children worry you?" "No." "Great Cwsar, man.l how's that?"

" Aint married ; live by Myself." UNEXPECTED PROOF. —Principal : "I have to send you on a very important errand, one demand- ing the greatest secrecy. Say, Meier, can I rely upon you? Are you able to keep a secret ?" Clerk : "Oh 1 certainly. (Whispering in prin- cipal's eat) : I have been secretly engaged to your daughter for the last couple of years !" A BEGGAR'S PE.AYER. —M. Pan, who has a very small nose, one day gave a coin to a poor fellow who, in thanking him, said :—" God preserve yosr sight." Why my sight ? " said M. Pan. "Because, if you happened to lose it, you could not wear any spectacles."

AFTER, a row with his wife, who violently expressed a wish that he was dead, an Irishman said: "Oh, it's a widow you're wantin' to be, is it? Bedad ! I'll take good care you're no widow as long as I live."

ADDING INSULT TO INJURY.—OId Lady: "That parrot I bought of you uses dreadful language." Bird Dealer: " Ah' mum, you should be werry careful what you ses afore it ; it's astonishite how quick them birds pick up anythink

Mrs. "Nov', grocer, you have charged me for things I've uever had. What de you mean by such items as one handful of raisins. one pocketful of almonds, two mouthfuls of sugar —eh?" Grocer: "it means, Mrs. Growler' that ladies who will bring their children with them when they do their marketin' has got to pay for all they gets." THAT INEVITABLE SNEER.—" How very studious she is !" said one girl. " Yea," replied the other. "She has ambitions. She wants to leave footprints in the sands of time." "Dear me ! I should think

she wouldn't." "What do you mean?" "They would be number seven, at least." A SOFT ANSWER.—Customer: "That umbrella

you gold me is made of such miserable poor stuff that it won't last a month." Dealer: " Yah. V. always sells dot kind to intellectual men like you. You gets to thinking on great subjects, and pecomes so absentminded you lose it in dree weeks; and den you hat de satisfaction off knowing dat de man who vinds it will get vet." HAD TO Co.—Young lady: "I must have some money to go off on a journey." Father: "Eh ? Where?" "I don't know yet, but I must go some- where at once." "God lands! What's happened ? " "The dressmaker misunderstood, and instead of a walking costume, she has made a going-away gown." A WEALTHY man displaying his jewels one day to a rhilosopher, the latter said : "Thank you, sir, for being willing to share such magnificent jewels with me." "Share them with you, sir? What do you mean ? " "Why, you allow me to look at them; and what more can you do with them yourself?" MAGISTRATE (to prisoner) : "You were caught in the act of opening a bedroom window." Prisoner: "Yes, your worship; I believe in hygiene, and I was only going to open the window an inch or two for the benefit of the occupant's health. It's frightfully unhealthy to sleep witlT your bedroom window completely shut up, your worship ! " A Goon story is told of a Quaker volunteer who was in a Virginia skirmish. Coming to pretty close quarters with a Southerner, he remarked, "Friend, it is unfortunate, but thee stands just. where I'm going to shoot." He ,blazed away, and down came the Southerner.

OLD LADY (in tears, to Chemist: " Wi—will you poison my dear lit—little Fido? He's in such —such agony! Chemist (politely) : " With pleasure, madam." Old Lady (indignantly) : " With pleasure, you nasty, unfeeling man! Then you shan't do it! " SCHOOLMATE : "Why do you never touch your piano ? " Miss Thumper: "We're buying it by instalments." " What differonce does that snake?" "I'm afraid if pa should hear me play, he'd stop paying." FOGGS : "Is the nine-eighteen train here yet?" Porter; "Twenty minutes late?" Foggs : "Wonld you mind telling my wife to wait if the tram gets here before I get back?" Porter '• But how am Ito know her?" Foggs : "Ah ! to be sure; I hadn't thought of that. WeU, tell her not to wait." A max going home from his work at a late hour at night, noticing that the occupants of a house standing flush with the street had lefts window up, decided to warn them and prevent a burglary. Putting his head into the window, he called out: hullo ! Good peop"—That was all he said. A whole pail of water struck him in the face, and, as he staggered back, a woman shrieked out: "Didn't I tell you what you'd get if %you wasn't home by nine o'clock?" A YOUNG man returned home a few days ago from a trip to South Africa for his health, and in narrating his adventures to his father, he told him he had bought a silver mine for £l,OOO. "I knew they'd swindle you," exclaimed the old man. "So you were fool enough to buy a humbug mine ? " " Yes ; but I didn't lose anything. I formed a company and sold half the stock to a Londoner for £1,500." "Y—you did?" gasped the old man, as he turned white. "I'll bet the one who bought it." "I know you are, coolly observed the young man, as he crossed his and tried to Annear very much at home.

"CAPITAL" CUA'.I

The liquor interest has hithe- te: seated in the House of Commons, at i6ist side of the brewers, but the licensed victuallere imselve9 have had for the most part -.a be sar' • with ex- erting their influence prps.ll.7 z , le. Now, however, one of the repr...:;,uteC c %.::.ans of the trade, the Central Protection Socio 4; has, through its chief officials, appealvl to the-Mirt;..',erial whips to put a licensed victu flerin noMo ,ion for the next available seat tiF ecknow.'..,,:od Conserva- tive candidate. Th .litiort is a Jasonable one, looking specially to -cailystrei.._•4',-ed to the Con- servatives by the vibli Ast election, and to the possibility o further Lib. J attacks in Par- liament upon the trade. The linisterial Whips hare acknowledge !Ate foto, of the claim, and a 1 licensed victualle win theit ore, be officially put forward for the first T ,ndon vacancy. This implies that the publicans „ a body will be now ranked as Conservatives, at least so long as the liquor question remains unsettled.

Some Conservative journals are hounding Lord Salisbury on to give effect to the Protectionist ideas prevalent in a large section of the party. They have reason to be bitter, and to feel a profound sense of disappointment at the see-saw of more than one of the Tory chiefs. But to win an election by holding out as a bait one set of political doctrines, and to carry out these doctrines when the victory is won, are two different things; and Lord Salis- bury has made plain enough his consciousness that if he introduces Pro•ection for one industry, he will have all the harassed or suffering interests pur- suing him like wolves, and calling out for similar treatment, while a rebellious nation of consumers would be rising in fury against him and his Govern- ment in every village and cottage in the land. Pro- tection for one means protection for all. A turther reason why Lord Salisbury dare not tamper with Protection is that his Government would not !last an hour if he did. Neither the Duke of Devonshire nor Mr. Chamberlain, nor their Liberal supporters throughout the country, have gone so far over to Toryism as to have renounced the doctrines of free trade.

I .......,. Some of the American papns excered themselves over the Marlborough wedding. Four or fl ve pages was the usual allowance of space, including illus- trations. "She is a Duchess Now; was the favourite head-line. Another was less polite: "A , 750.000 Dollar Ceremony," it ran, with, for a second line, 'Society Played Tag with Itself in the Effort , to Set the Affair." The "New York Herald" had ,1 half a column of headings, including, "The Bishop ' szet Funny, and the Bridegroom Made a Rattling Americut Speech.' It is said that live hundred dollars Was offered for one of the special passes tor admission to the church.

The Duke's family solicitor, Mr. Harding Mil- ward, was a hard nut for the American reporter, who were especially anxious to find out the facts about the marriage settlements. Mr. Milward, naturally, would not accommodate them. So they went back to their respective offices, and penned numerous fiction. The Duke has refused to touch a shiiling of the bride's money, which is settled on her in the ordinary way. To the other question, " What do you think of the country?" Mr. Milward had an invarable answer: " E am lost in amaze- ment."

The new Commander-in-Chief has taken a some- what important step with recard to uniforms. Hitherto, in the opinion of many, British officers have not shown sufficient pride in their profession, inasmuch as the instant they are off duty they hasten to discard every outward and visible sign of soldiering, A first step in the right direction has been taken by Lord Wolsely, who has ordained that all officers on full pay attending his levees shall appear in uniform.

The hero of Mr. Stead's new Chfstmos number of the "Review of Reviews" is Mr. Chamberlain. And the fate which Mr. Stead metes out to that qtatesmen—who is hardly diAguised under the pun- ning title " Blastus," the King's Chamberlain "—is that of the Leader of the Liberal party and Prime Minister of a Liberal Administration. This is a re- velation, but no one would guess the fate that Mr. Stead reserves for Lord Rosebery. The ex-premier is visible on page 96 of the Annual, being crowned King of Israel by Lord Rothschild, Baron Hirsch, and Mr. Barney Barnato I Mr. Stead chronicles that "no event of recent times provoked anything approaching the enthusiastic interest with which this strange coronation was watched throughout Giristendorn." Strange to say he does no describe the feeling with which Mr. Labonchere regarded the fulfilment of this more than bierselian dream.

To put; an end to all the gossip about Prince Itenr3 of Battenburg, it is stated that he goes out to Ashanti purely as a volunteer, without rank, pay, re• allowances. He is, in fact, going, R. 9 the Army saying has it, "for a medal or a bullet." But he would appear to come under the War Office deci- sion, invented for tte special behoof ot correspon- dents, that. medals canrot he given te gentlemen who join an expedition "for their own purposes."

The September issue of the " Come Monthly" from the little settlement in dis,ant Paraguay gives a most. exhauative account within a brief Qpncl- of Colonia Cosme. The weather is reported as variable, health as better than usual, and diet as wholly vegetable. School has been open every 11 y, dancing goes on vigorously, and literature of a hioh character is being read. Seeds of every sort and kind are needed, and may he sent to Mr. W. G. ponce, of Castlereagh-at., Sydney, N.S.W. The olono, little organ says distinctly that nobody should dream of going to Colonia Come who d3es not +incept life-marriage, the colour-line. and total eiatinence from strong drink among &finite prin- ciples, and who does not hold Cnmrnunism "to be not merely expedient hut right," The strength of public opinion and the necessity for self-re=traint are the two most powerful social forces which dominate the colony's life.

I bear from Woolwich that although all the stores for the Ashantee expedition have been des- patched, abnormal activity continues there. Vigor- ous Pffortil are being made to get up our stores of ammunition, and the fine machinery in the Arsenal which does this work is turning out great quantities driiir. There is no disguising the fact that the out- look is regarded among military men as anything hut peaneful, and they point ta this activity at Woolwich as bearing them out in that opinion.

Extraordinary efforts are being made to prevent, distinguished names being mentioned in connection ! with the inquiry that is now being made into the Operations of a certain matrimonial agency. It en- pears a certain gentleman of the highest public standing was married some live years ego through the Medium of the ngency,and it is but natural that he should not he desirous to have his name men- tioned. The judge in the case, as well as the solici- tors for both parties, have, it is understood. been appealed to with a view to the suppression of the name in question. --- A proposal will he made at the coming Wesleyan Conference to inflict a heavy fine on ministers who leave the Metbodist, Church. The curious idea of preventing secession appears to have been suggested by the action of the Rev. F. Ballard.who has lately become a Congregationalist. end early next year enters upon the pastorate of Wycliffe Church.lTull. It is, however. exceedingly unlikely that the reso- lution threatened will he carried, though it , may possibly ttive rise to an animated discussion.

There i 4 no denying that Mr.Morlev's expression O f confidence in Lord Salishur:y's Turkiah nolicv have caused hoth'surprise and consternation. People are asking how it crimes to pass that, just when Liberals are nrving for n Cromwell who will avenge the PI ilgh tPred Christians of Armenia, and when even Con.ervatives are beginning to b. impatient under Lord Salisbury's helplessneas. Mr. Morley elould rival the language of Toryism in hailing our Foreign Secretary's impotence as proof that be is a heaven-born Minister.

The testimonial to Mr. Middleton, the chief Tory agent, set afoot with such a flourish four months ago.im not doing very well. One Cabinet Minister has damned it with the slender contribution of a five-pound note, and the number of wealthy mem- bers of the party who confined their expression of admiration within the limits of a modest guinea rune into hundreds. It is the same with the Tory , associations through the country. Twelve thousand pounds was subscribed for Mr. Schnadhorst, and, considering the wealth of the " gentlemen of Eng- lend." they ought surely t,-.1 have done better than £5,000, the present total.

A peculiar Puritan•like sentence was imposed on three offenders in Norristown, Pennsylvania, a few days ago. They were accused of assault and battery, and were re- leased on their promise to accompany their accuser to church on each of the twelve next succeeding Sundays.

Winter Skin Diseases.

Dr. William Thomas Corlett reports some diseases of the skin which appear in winter and disappear in sum- mer. Doubtless many persons have suffered from such affections. Such diseases are directly due to cold. They are characterised by variously-sized, round and horseshoe-shaped patches, which are sometimes markedly thickened, and having an abrupt, well-defined margin and of a dusky red colour. Later the patch takes on a faded rose coloured hue and becomes covered with a thin layer of adherent scales. Itching may or may not be present.

Liu)lES' LETTER.

AY DEAREST NELLIE,

In Paris velveteen is all the rage, and it will nob be long before it is here. Winter mohair is decidedly taking a large part in the new styles, especially for skirts. One reason is that it goes so very well with blouses of velvet or velveteen. I may add that here velveteen blouses and gowns are very fashionable, for fashion is no longer a one-sided affair. If the Parisiennes lead the way in dainty styles, they are constantly sending to England for severer cuts and their specialities in materials. Velveteen will be as fashionable in Paris as are already the English zloths, tweeds and sanitary underclothing, especially velveteens in sof t old-fashioned shades (once called xsthetic in London) like sage-green, old gold, heliotrope, puce, peacock, antique blue, putty-colour (beige) and certain tints of brown.

As for jackets, they are, as you know, much looser than they were. One in the very newest style was made in bright natural brown homespun,

with straight, loose fronts, and fitting back termi- nating in short full basques. The sharply cut collar was faced with brown velvet, and the big buttons are of pebble matching the cloth. It is some time since I said anything about even- ing dresses, so I will take the opportunity of giving you a little " jaw " upon them to-day. Here, then, is a 'very pretty dress for those to whom the very décolleté style of dress does not appeal. The material chosen for this charming gown was a thin, pink satin foulard of a pretty floral design; the skirt was quite plain, and must be lined, if possible, with silk or a cheap cotton-backed satin. White would be the most effective lining, and white silk or satin is often to be found at a much lower price than a colour, and as ten yards will amply suffice to line the skirt, and will leave cuttings from which, with care, both linings of bodice and sleeves may be fashioned, it will not be found an extrava- gant match. The bodice is of brocade also, and the straps of ribbon, falling to the feet, a style I can cordially recommend to any lady whose length of waist leaves something to be desired, are of tender lettuce green, the accordion-pleated shoulder capes of chiffon, which daintily veil the sleeves, being of the same colour. Plumes and wings are still the most prominent parts of our headgear, and the latter are somewhat snore fashionable than the former. I confess I' don't like the prevalent fashion at all, as ribbons and flowers are, I think, much prettier; and one feels that the feather-crowned hat somehow wants an "Ich diene " underneath instead of a pretty face ! However, one has to give in to fashion, however vulgar she may be sometimes, so here is one of the hats I have just been complaining of. It has a crown of that new material, felt and straw plaited together. This crown is of green

and the brim is of black velvet with bunches of violets in the front, and at the sides two natural. tinted feathers, while in the centre are two black plumes standing up. I must not forget to tell you of some of the beautiful dresses Felix has made for Mme. Emma Nevada for her American tour. Besides costumes for "Marguerite," one in pink cloth, with a corselet embroidered with ox-eyed daisies and steel spangles, the other in black embroidered with jet, there is a "Mignon" costume which is well worthy of a description. It is in red velvet, the knicker- bockers enclosed in the gaiters; a blouse of velvet of the same colour with long basques embroidered in steel, and the front of this blouse in grey satin spangled with silver. A most becoming costume to its piquante wearer. A very pretty dress is in almond veloutine. The bodice draped over a square of embroidered chenille and spangles. The novelty in the skirt is in a drapery on one side attached to the waist by a jewelled lizard. Madame Nevada will be heard in all her repertoire on the other side of the Atlantic, and I hear that the Americans are eagerly looking forward to her visit.

What jolly little affairs children's garments are, and this year they seem more charming than ever. Here is an effective little coat made of a turquoise blue diagonal cloth, with the collar edged with

lynx. The hat is of white silk, with the full crown striped with silk passementerie, rosettes of baby ribbon clustering round the back, while in the front is a double bow.

Your loving JENNis.

A Taste For Medicine.

There is no accounting for taste. The triteness of this observation is emphasised by a correspondent of the "Chemist and Druggist," who informs us that the Basu- to are 20 inordinately fond of medicine that they drink it as a relish. The officer in charge of the public dispen- sary at one of the chief posts in the Protectorate reports that he has been compelled to make a charge of Bd. for out-patients, in order to "weed oir, the cast.s that only attended out of pure love of physic-drinking." We pre- sume the Basutos found the hospital draughts an im- provement on their usual tipple of Cape brandy.

Shawls.

If we are to be credulous of statements in the penny papers, the Queen has now been able to get rid—at one gift—of about five hundred of her famous Indian shawls. It is announced that each of the wives of King %llama and the Bechuanaland chiefs has received as a present the Eastern mantle so dear to the fine ladies of 1840. Now, as no self-respecting African monarch would con- skier himself a domestic person without ewning at least one hundred and fifty wives, it will be seen at a glance what a large output of cashmeres there will be this week from the cupboards at Windsor. For my part, writes Ella Hepworth Dixon in the " Lady's Pictorial," I like to think of those hundreds of dusky ladies in Bechuana- land arraying themselves presently in priceless Indian shawls. Will they wear them as" La Dame aux Came- has" used to do, with one fringed point touching the ground.or as a plaidie, thrown over one shoulder,or as a covering for the head P Will they wear any other gar- ment with it.or merely the local airy costume of fringes and beads ? Will the exigencies of the appropriate corn- pell them to revive with the Indian shawl, the crinoline, the poke bonnet, and the dome-shaped parasol P Once upon a time it was an all-conquering garment, this shawl pinned across the bosom with a vast brooch, and ladies of the half-world and whole world vied with each other in the elegance with which they wore it over their tumultuusly-flounced petticoats. Together with dia- monds, fursonci old lace, it formed an integral part of the corbeille offered by the briiegroom to the bride at all genteel French marriages. These costly mantles— which now, often as not, du duty as piano covers in suburban parlours—are sent as an annual tribute from India to the Empress Queen.

Care of the Eyes.

The care needed to be given to our eyes is yearly be- coming more apparent. The first step in canine for the eyes is to use them but sparingly. The strain of steady and continuous work is generally injurious even to strong eyes. The woman who has to use her eyes steadily should give them frequent, even if brief, vaca- tions. If she has to write all day, or if she sews con- tinuously, she should give her eyes five-minute resting spells every two hours. Dropping the work. closing the eyes, and keeping them closed for even this brief tirm-, rests net only the eyes but the brain, and the work is easier and more inspiring afterwards. Those who are onliged to use the eyes all day at business should not use them more than is necessary in the evening. Fine sew- ing, reading, and writing should be quite eschewed. Very hot water is beneficial to the eyes it rightly applied. It counteracts any tendency to inflammation, and rests and relaxes the muscles about the eyes. It should be applied not merely against the lids but to the eyes themselves. Water may also be dashed against the eyes. Five minutes of the hot-water treatment at the close of a day's work, followed by five minutes' rest with eyes closed, does wonders towards refreshing tired eyes. A very frequent prescription for weak or slightly. inflamed eyes is " bi-borate of sodium and aqua pure," which, being intrepreted, is simply "borax and pure water." Use soft water and but little borax. Great care must be taken with eyelashes, for they not Only are necessary for the harmonious beauty of the face, but also for the protection of the eyes. They are much favoured by a mixture of petro-vaseline liquid, mixed with boricated water. A cloth wet with the so- lution is applied to the eyes.

In Defence of the Lady Bicyclist.

" I have seen," says an eminent French physician, "women obliged to change all the armholes of their gowns,so great was the muscular development of the top of their shoulders. Another fact observable to day is that enlarged muscles can be retained until advanced age._

" Bicycling imparts to woman's walk more assurance. One feels in her presence confidence in her strength. " Her walk hitherto has often been uncertain, irregu- lar. She would hesitate to advance or pass in haste in a dangerous place, and would run the greatest risks be- cause the did not know how to measure the danger. Bicycling, he says, puts her on the alert. The neces- sity of not being knocked down teaches her very quickly to direct herself with assurance, and to perceive at a glance all the obstacles of the way. Begun at an early age, the continuous exercise of the bicycle will have a marked influence on the judgment and the character. The bicycle, he tells his readers, reduces flesh first, because the exercise in itself preserves combustion in proportion to the material consumed. Women who preserve sufficient activity are certainly less prone to premature corpulency, while those who are not corpul- ently inclined, but indulge in bad hygiene, find that the bicycle obliges them to take better care of themselves. They will be better able to support increased flesh when the proper period for it arrives. But one ought not to count too much on reducing flesh by the use of the bizycle he assures his readers. When thinness is desired through active exercise, a special regime is imperative. In short, this erudite Frenchman, who is evidently a veteran bicyclist, sees in the legitimate, reasonable exer- cise of the wheel the physical and moral regeneration of woman,and through her mankind.

'THE HIDDEN HAND;

CAPITOLA THE MADCAP. BY Ma. E. D. E. N. SOUTHWORTIL

CHAPTER XI.

a MYSTERY AND A STORM AT HURRICANE HALL, "Bid her address her silent prayers to Heaven! Learn if she there may be forgiven; It's mercy may absolve her yet! But here upon this earth beneath, There is no spot where she and I Together for an hour could breathe 1" —Butorr. 'Early the next morning Capitola arose, made her toilet, and went out to explore the outer walls her part of the old house, to discover, if pos- sible, some external entrance into the unknown ..e.vity under her room. It was a bright, cheerful, healthy, autumnal morning, well adapted to dispel sill clouds of mystery and superstition. Heaps of crimson and golden-hued leaves, glimmering with hoar frost, lay drifted against the old walls, and :svhen these were brushed away by the busy feet and hands of the young girl, they revealed nothing but the old mouldering foundations; not a vestige of,a cellar-door or window was visible. r Capitols abandoned the fruitless search, and turned to go into the house. And saying to her- self:

m "I'll think no more of it ! I dare say, after all, it 'is nothing but a very dark cellar without window and with a well, and the story of the murders and of the skeletons is all moonshine I" She ran into the dining-room, and took her seat at the breakfast table.

v- Old Hurricane was just then storming away at his factotum Wool for some misdemeanour, the mature of whicli Capitola did not hear, for upon her appearance, he suffered his wrath to subside in a few reverberating low thunders, gave his ward a grumpy "Good-morning," and sat down to his breakfast. After breakfast Old Hurricane took his great coat and cocked hat, and stormed forth upon the plantation to blow up his lazy overseer, Mr. Will V_,zy, and his idle negroes, who had frolicked away all the days of their master's absence. r. Mrs. Condiment went away to mix a plum pud- ding for dinner, and Cap was left alone. k. lifter wandering through the lower rooms of the house, the stately old-fashioned drawing-room, the family parlour, the dining-room' &c., Cap found her way through all the narrow back passages and steep little stair-cases back to her own chamber. 40 The chamber looked quite different by daylight —the cheerful wood fire burning in the chimney right before her, opposite the door by which she entered; the crimson curtained bedstead on her right hand; the crimson draped windows, with the rich old mahogany bureau and dressing-glass standing between them, on her left; the polished, dark oak floor; the rich Turkey rug, concealing the trap-door; the comfortable rocking-chair; the new workstand, placed there for her use that morning, and her own well-filled trunks standing in the corners, looked altogether too cheerful to associate with dark thoughts. Besides, Capitola had not the least particle of gloom, superstition, or marvellousness in her dis- position. She loved old houses and old legends well enough to enjoy them, but was not sufficiently credulous to believe, or cowardly to fear, them. She had, besides, a pleasant morning's occupation before her, in unpacking her three trunks and arranging her wardrobe and her possessions, which were all upon the most liberal scale, for Major Warfield at every city where they had stopped had given his poor little protege' a virtual carte blanche for purchases, having said to her: "Capitola, I'm an old bachelor; I've not the least idea what a young girl requires; all I know is, that you have nothing but your clothes, and must want sewing and knitting needles, and brushes and scissors, and combs and boxes and smelling-bottles and tooth-powder; and such. So come along with me to one of those Vanity Fairs they call fancy stores, and get what you want; foot the bill."

And Capitola, who firmly believed that she had the most sacred of claims upon Major Warfield, whose resources she also supposed to be unlimited, did not fail to indulge her taste for rich and costly toys, and supplied herself with a large ivory dres- sing-case, lined with velvet, and furnished with ivory-handled combs and brushes, silver boxes and crystal bottles; a papier mache workbox, with gold thimble, needle-case and perforator and gold. mounted scissors and winders; and an ebony writing-desk, with silver-mounted crystal stall. dishes; each of these—boxes and desk—were filled 'with all things requisite in the several departments. And now as Capitola unpacked them and arranged them upon the top of the bureau, it was with no small degree of appreciation. The rest of the fore. noon was spent in arranging the best articles of her wardrobe in her bureau drawers.

Having locked the remainder in her trunks, and carefully smoothed her hair, and dressed herself in a brown merino, she went downstairs and sought out Mrs. Condiment, whom she found in the house- keeper's little room, and to whom she said: "Now, Mrs. Condiment, if uncle has any needle- work wanted to be done, any buttons to be sewed on, or anything of the kind, just let me have it; I'm just dying to use it!" "My dear Miss Black—" "Please to call me Capitola., or even Cap. I never was called Miss Black in my life, until I came here, and I don't like it at all." _ _ _ _ _

"Well then, my dear Misa Cap, I wish you would wait till to-morrow, for I just came in here in a great hurry to get a glass of brandy out of the cupboard to put in the sauce for theplum-pudding, as dinner will be on the table in ten minutes."

With a shrug of her little shoulders, Capitola left the housekeeper's room, and hurried through the central front hall and out at the front door, to look about and breathe the fresh air for a while.

As she stepped upon the front piazza she saw Major Warfield walkin z up the steep lawn, fol- lowed by Wool, leading a pretty, mottled, iron- grey pony, with a side-saddle on his back. " Ah, I'm glad you're down, Cap! Come! look at this pretty posy! he is good for nothing as a working horse, and i too light to carry my weight, and so I intend to give him to you ! You must learn to ride," said the old man, coming up the steps. "Give him to me! I learn te ride I Oh, uncle? Oh, uncle! I shall go perfectly crazy with joy ?" exclaimed Cap, dancing and clapping her hands with delight. "Oh, well, a tumble or two in learning will bring you back to your senses, I reckon I" "Oh, uncle! oh, uncle ! when shall I begin !" "You shall take your first tumble immediately after dinner, when, being well-filled, you will not be so brittle and apt to break in falling ?" "Oh, uncle ! I shall not fall! I feel I sha'n't I I feel I've a natural gift for holding on I" "Come, come, get in! get in! I want my dinner !" said Old Hurricane, driving his ward in before him to the dining, room, where the dinner was smoking upon the table. After dinner Cap, with Wool for a riding-master, took her first lesson in equestrianism. She had the four great requisites for forming a good rider—a well-adapted figure, a fondness for the exercise, perfect fearlessness, and presence of mind. She was not once in danger of losing her seat, and during that afternoon's exercises, she make considerable progress in learning to manage her steed.

Old Hurricane, whom the genial autumn after- noon had tempted out to smoke his pipe in his arm-chair on the porch, was a pleased spectator of her performances, and expressed his opinion that in time se would become the best rider in the neighbourhood, and that she should have the best 'riding dress and cap that could be made at Tip- Top. Just now, in lack of an equestrian dress, poor Cap was parading around and around the lawn with her head bare and her hair flying, and her merino skirt exhibiting more ankles than grace. It was while Old Hurricane still sat smoking his pipe and making his comments and Capitol& still ambled around and around the lawn, that a horse- man suddenly appeared galloping as fast as the steep nature of the ground would admit, up towards the house, and before they could form an idea of who he was, the horse was at the block, and the rider dismounted and standing before Major Wartield. "Why, Herbert, my boy! back so soon ! We didn't expect you for a week to come. This is sudden, indeed. So much the better, so much the better. Glad to see you, lad !" exclaimed Old Hurricane, getting up and heartily shaking the ' hand of his nephew. Capitola came ambling up, and in the effort to spring nimbly from her saddle, tumbled off, much to the delight of Wool, who grinned from ear to ear, and of Old Hurricane, who, with an "1 said so," burst into a roar of laughter. Herbert Greyson sprang to assist her ; but before I he reached the spot, Cap had picked herself up, I straightened her disordered dress, and now she milt° meet and shake hands with him.

There was such a sparkle of joy and glow of affection in the meeting between these two that Old Hurricane, who saw it, suddenly hushed his laugh, and grunted to himself: Humph, humph, humph! I like that; that's better than I could have planned it myself; let that go on, and then Gabe Le Noir, we'll see under what name and head the old divided manor will be held !"

Before his mental soliloquy was concluded, Herbert and Capitola came up to him. He wel- comed Herbert again with great cordiality, and then called to his man to put up the horses, and bade the young people follow him Into the houses, as the air was getting chilly. "And how did you find your good friends, lad ?" inquired Old Hurricane, when they had reached the sitting parlour. "Oh, very well, sir; and very grateful for your offered kindness; and indeed, so anxious to express their gratitude that—that I shortened my visit, and came away immediately to tell you." "Right, lad, right I You came by the down coach 7" "Yes, sir; and got off at Tip-Top, where I hired a horse to bring me here. I must ask you to let one of your men take him back to Mr. Merry, at the Antlers' Inn, to-morrow." " Strely, surely, lad! Wool shall do it 1" "And so, Herbert, the poor woman was de- lighted with the prospect of better times ?" said Old Hurricane, with a little glow of benevolent self-satisfaction.

"Oh, yes, sir! delighted beyond all measure 1" "Poor thing! poor thing! See, young folks, Low easy it is for the wealthy, by sparing a little of their superfluous means, to make the poor and virtuous happy. And the boy, Herbert, the boy ?" "Oh, sir, delighted for himself, but still more delighted for his mother; for her joy was such as to astonish and even alarm me 1 Before that, I had thought Marah Rocke a proud woman, but—" " WHAT—say that again I" exclaimed Major Warfield.

"I say that I thought she was a proud woman, but--"

"Thought WHO was a proud woman, air ?"roared Old Hurricane.

" Marah Rocke !" replied the young man, with wonder.

Major Warfield started up, seized the chair upon which he had sat, and struck it upon the ground with such force as to shatter it to pieces; then turning, he strode up and down the floor with such violence that the two young people gazed after him in consternation and fearful expectancy. Presently he turned suddenly, strode up to Herbert Greyson, and stood before him.

His face was purple, his veins were swollen until they stood out upon his forehead like cords, his eyes were protruded and glaring, his mouth clenched until the grizzly grey moustache and beard were drawn in, his whole huge frame was quivering from head to foot! It was impossible to tell what passion—whether rage, grief, or shame, the most possessed him, for all three seemed tear- ing his giant frame to pieces. For an instant he stood speechless, and Herbert feared he would fall into a fit; but the old giant was too strong for that! For one short moment he stood thus, and in a terrible voice he asked : "Young man! did you—did you know—the SHAME that you dashed in my face with the name of that woman ?"

"Sir, I know nothing but that she is the best and dearest of her sex !" exclaimed Herbert, beyond all measure amazed at what he heard and saw. "Best and dearest!" thundered the old man— "oh, idiot! is she still a siren, and are you a dupe? But that cannot be! No, sir lit is I whom you both would dupe! Ah, I see it all now I This is why you artfully concealed her name from me until you had won my promise! It shall not serve either you or her, sir! I break my promise—thus I" —bending and snapping his own cane, and flinging the fragments behind his back—" there, sir ! when you can make those ends of dry cedar grow to- gether again, and bear green leaves, you may hope to reconcile Ira Warfield and Marsh Rocke I I break my promise, sir, as she broke " The old man suddenly sunk back into the nearest chair, dropped his chaggy head and face into him hands, and remained trembling from head to toot, while the convulsive heaving of his chest, and the rising and falling of his huge shoulders, betrayed that his heart was nearly bursting with such sup pressed sobs as only can be forced from manhood by the fiercest anguish. The young people looked on in wonder, awe, and pity; and then their eyes met—those Of Herbert silently inquired : "What can all this mean ?"—Those of Capitols as mutely answered: "Heaven only knows." In his deep pity for the old man's terrible anguish, Herbert could feel no shame nor resentment for the false accusation made upon himself. Indeed, his noble and candid nature easily explained all as the ravings of some heart-rending remembrance. Waiting, therefore, until the violent convulsions of the old man's frame had somewhat subsided, Herbert went to him, and with a low and respectful intonation of voice, said: "Uncle, if you think that there was any collu- sion between myself and Mrs. Rocke, you wrong us both. You will remember that -when I met you in New York, I had not seen or heard from her for years, nor had I then any expectation of ever seeing you. The subject of the poor widow came up between us accidentally, and if it is true that I omitted to call her by name, it must have been because we both then felt too tenderly by her to call her anything else but the poor widow, the poor mother, the good woman,' and so on—and all this she is still." The old man without raising his head, held out one hand to his nephew, saying in a voice trembling with emotion "Herbert, I wronged you; forgive me." Herbert took and pressed that rugged and hairy old hand to his lips, and said: "Uncle, I do not in the least know what is the cause of your present emotion, but—" " Emotion ! demmy, sir! what do you mean by emotion! Am / a man to give way to emotion Demmy, sir, mind what you say !" roared the old lion, getting up and shaking himself free of all weaknesses. "I merely meant to say, air, that if I could pos- sibly be of any service to you, I am entirely at your orders." "Then go back to that woman and tell her never to dare to utter, or even to think my name again, If she values her life !"

- "Sir, you do not mean it! and as for Mrs. Rocke, she is a good woman I feel it my duty to uphold 1" " Good ! ugh! ugh! ugh! I'll command myself! I'll not give way again. Good! ah,.lad, it is quite plain to me now, that you are an Innocent dupe. Tell me now, for instance, do you know anything of that woman's life before she came to reside at Staunton ?"

"Nothing; but from what I've seen of her since, I'm sure she always was good." "Did she never mention her former life at a 112" " Never ; but, mind! I hold to my faith in her and would stake my salvation on her integrity," said Herbert, warmly. "Then you'd lose it, lad, that's all; but I have an explanation to make to you, Herbert. You must give me a minute or two of your company alone, in the library, before tea." And so saying, Major Warfield arose and led the way across the hall to the library, that was imme- diately back of the drawing-room.

Throwing himself into a leathern chair beside the writing table, he motioned for his companion to take the one on the opposite side. A low fire smouldering on the hearth before them, so dimly lighted the room, that the young man arose again to pull the bell rope; but the other interrupted with: "No, you need not ring for lights, Herbert; my story is one that should be told in the dark! listen, lad • but drop your eyes the while! am all attention, sir !" "Herbert I the poet says that: 'At thirty man suspects himself a fool, Knows it at forty, and reforms his rule.' But, boy, at the ripe age of forty-five I succeeded in achieving the most sublime folly of my life! I should have taken a degree in madness, and been raised to a professor's chair in some College of Lunacy ! Herbert, at the age of forty-five I fell in love and married a girl of sixteen, out of a log cabin! merely, forsooth, because she .tad a pretty skin like the leaf of the white japonica, soft, grey eyes like a timid fawn's and a voice like a cooing turtle dove's ! because those delicate cheeks flushed and those soft eyes fell when I spoke to her, and the cooing voice trembled when she replied! because the delicate face brightened when I came, and faded when I turned away because 'She wept with delight when I gave her a smile, And trembled with fear at my frown,' etc. Because she adored me as a sort of god, I loved her as an angel, and married her! married her secretly! for fear of the ridicule of my brother officers, put her in a pastoral log cabin in the woods below the block-house, and visited her there by stealth, like NANA aid hi& uymph_ i the_ guye. But I watt

Watched, my hiaaen treasure was discovered— coveted by a younger and prettier fellow than my- self Perdition ! I cannot tell this story In detail! One night I came home —ery late and quite unexpectedly, and found—this man in nIY wife's cabin! I broke the man's head and ribs And left him for dead. I tore the woman out of my heart and cauterised its bleeding wounds !—This man was Gabriel Le Noir ! Satan burn him for- ever !—This woman was Marah Rocke, God forgive her! I could have divorced the woman, but as I did not dream of ever marrying again. I did nob care to drag my shame before a public tribunal; There, you know all I let the subject sink for ever I said Old Hurricane, wiping great drops of sweat from his labouring brows. " Uncle ! I have heard your story and believe you, of course But lam bound to tell you that, without having heard your poor wife's defence, I believe, and uphold her to be innocent I I think you have been as grossly deceived as she has been fearfully wi onged I and that time and providence will prove this !" exclaimed Herbert, fervently. A horrible laugh of scorn was his only answer* as Old Hurricane arose, shook himself and led the way back to the parlour. (To be tontinued.)

LADY THROWN FROM A TRAP.

The slight mischance of a fall from a pony trap had serious results in the case of Mrs. Devonshire, a well- known and widely-respected resident in the little town of Beaconsfield. The accident occurred some eix yea" ago, and seemed to inflict a severe nervous shock. No particular part, as she felt then, was injured, but for almost a twelvemonth she wasted away and got ahnoat like a shadow. She began to have a very strange feel- ing, from her back right down to the bottom of her feet' She was examined by several doctors and went into one of the London hospitals for over two months,but did not get better. She could net turn in bed, could not move, nor could she get upstairs. The hospital doctors did not seem to understand her case at all. Abscesses formed in her side, and the doctor said that explained all that she bad suffered before. She afterwards went to the High Wycombe and Earl of Beaconsfield MeV:44lll, Cottage Hospital—receiving excellent treatment...442U lay prostrate for a twelvemonth. But no permanent re- lief was obtained until some time afterwards when she tried Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. "In the "Christmas Globe." The case we saw ?Ms 3 similar one to mine. I had six boxes at first. I sent to Dr. Williams' establishment in London." "You look well now."

" And I feel well. I can get about now and do WY work. I seemed to gain more strength at once when I began with the Pills, and could move better. It was the Pills that did it, without doubt. When I began to get about, everybody was telling me what a miracle it was' and making such remarks as I never thought yod would get about any more,' 'You are a miracle,' Z=ol°' Everyone was astonished." Various people in Beaconsfield, on whom the reporter called, corroborated Mrs. Devonshire's statements, and expressed their surprise at her being able to get about again. Alderman SUTTON alluded Cc the letting of the shop at; Bridge-foot, at one time occupied by the bate Mr. IL W. 'Marsh, which he said had been decided upon by the committee though it was not mentioned on the printed minutes. The Street Improvement 'Committee had ' passed a resolution to the efiect that the shop should not be re-let for the sale of goods, which it was considered, weuld act detrimentally to the shopkeepers. He considered the committee were •quite right to let the showier the present. _ Councillor PIERPOINT thought the remarks of council- lor Hepherd savoured of the ridiculous. If theUorpora- tion wished to encourage motlvauctions to the detriment of the tradesmen of the town fur tho purpose of saving a paltry £3 a week he was very much ashamed of them. SANITARY WORKS COUNITTEE.TRE LOSSOFORD EXTENSION.

At a'meeting of this committee held on the 27th -November, it was resolved that the Town 'Clerk apply to the Local Government Board for power to borrow £15,000 for extensions at Longford Depot. Councillor Mottos, in moving the confirmation of the ,minutes, alluded to the proposed borrowing ef £15,000. He mentioned that he hadforeshadowed early this year a further expenditure at'Lengford in connection with the extension. He had purposely allowed the matter to drift somewhat of late feeling that it was desirable, in view of other expenditure to which the Council would be com- mitted, to let the matter stand over as long as possible, but the time had now come 'when her and 'his committee felt that it was necessary-there should be-no further de- gay. They were prepating a detailed scheme to lay before the Council, and it would he brought forward in three or four months' time. Thay wouldpuot be able to apply to the Local Government Board until the 30th 'November next year unless they acted at once, and at the suggestion of the Town Clerk the committee at the last meeting passed a resolution to the effect that appli- cation should be made to the Local Government Board for power to borrow the money. The committee, how- ever, could not spend the money until they 'had drawn up a scheme and submitted it to the Council. Then an inspector from the local 'Government Board would be sent to Warrington to inquire into the matter. Councillor Homo seconded, _ _

Councillor Sammons', said he would object to the 'expenditure. The MigrOß remarked that the money would not be spent at present. Councillor Saarrannsr said he had learnt since he became a member of the Council that when money was borrowed it was sure to be spent. There was no question about that. They had already spent without sanction £4,000. They were now about to borrow £1:6,000, and the £4,000 would be paid out of that sum. A definite statement as to the manner in which the money would be spent should be submitted to the Council before the loan was sanctioned. .

Councillor Woons 'took objection to the expenditure of £l5O in the purchase of a couple of horses for the use of the department. They should not, he contended, have cost more than £5O each.

Councillor Snaw GREEN pointed out that before the Sum of £15,000 was borrowed the Local Goverment Board would have to be consulted and a detailed state- ment prepared. Councillor ROBERTS said that as he had something to do with the recommendation for the purchase of the horses be should like to say that he would not advise the Corporation to pay more for a horse than he would pay himself. He was in the habit of paying the same price, or a similar price, to that paid by the committee, and he believed that the best horses paid the best. (Hear, hear.) It was discreditable to a Corporation to turn out shabby horses. Alluding to the proposed loan of £15,000. he said he was surprised that Councillor Smethurst should Oppose it since the matter had been discussed so of ten in committee. The increased expenditure was absolutely necessary because of the increased quantities of material which had to be dealt with at Longford. Councillor Monts said in regard to Councillor Smet- hurst's opposition that it was true the committee had spent a certain sum of money, but they had done so with the knowledge and consent of the Council. Coun- cillor Smethurst was present at the time and raised no objection. Councillor SMETHURST I beg your pardon, sir. Councillar MONKS said he would repeat the assertion. Not a word was said in any shape or form. If Council- lor Smethurst did not approve, he did not get up and object; in fact, all along he (Councillor Monks) had not brought forward any motion without feeling certain that he would have the support of every member of the com- mittee. Whether that would be so in the future re- mained to be seen, but he hoped that support would be continued. This was a preliminary application, and they could do nothing in the matter of spending money without coming before the Council again. He gave his guarantee that nothing should be done until the Council had been again consulted. (Hear, hear.) The minutes were then passed. ATIIEST 11CFROVEMENT CONIMITTEE—THE STOCKTON HEATH

SEWAGE.

Alderman WRIGHT proposed the adoption of the minutes of this committee, and said with regard to the report concerning the sewerage of Stockton Heath that for a long also loud complaints had been raised about the stench arising from the bed of the old river at the back of Wilderspool Causeway, and they were anxious to get rid of that. They had, therefore, worked out a scheme, and all that the Council would be asked to do that night was to adopt the general principle of it. Before they applied to the Local Government Board for their approval they would have to wait until the exten- sion of the borough was granted. It would be, perhaps, advisable to give the Council an epitome of the circum- stances which led up to the present position. It would be in the recollection of the Council that arrangements were made with the Runcorn District Council that upon the oxtension of the borouv.h being granted the Corpora- tion would deal with the sewage from Stockton Heath, which was now discharged in the bend of the old river at Wildersp,ol. The Bill for the extension had been read a second time in the House, but owing to the dis- solution it was suspended until the reassembling of Parliament in February last. ENGL A.N D'S FOOD SUPPLY.

EBY Mn. H. WHITII,CTI.

One of the most "burning questions," andl,cne that has filled hundreds of columns in the British newspapers during the past twenty, or thirty years, has been the "Land Question." Bookspam- phlets, and articles without number have been written upon this one topic. ,How to make the best use of I the land; how tolarm at a profit, and to feed the ever-growing urban population, have been the problems that have to be solved. 'Seem- ingly we are not much nearerthe solution of these questions to-day than we were years ago. Nay, all the evidecce to hand would prove that we are fur- ther off than ever. I can find reports from almost every shire proving that more land is, day by day, going out of cultivation, and year after year the importakons of food stuffs is increasing. The interest in this question is not alone con- fined to the farmer and the • land owner. dt is of I national importance. The wage earner in the fac- tory has an interest in the land, employing all the labour te possibly can. He has also an interest in the land at his door providing him with as cheap food as possible. Whatever •" 'ism" we may look upon with especial favour, or whatever political flag we may swear by, there can be no doubt that we are all held pretty close to the grindstone by the competitive system coder which we are living, and that we must make ,the best of it. It is just possible that one of these days the farmer and the farm band, the artisan and the labourer, will wake up to the fact that to put trust' in Pailiament is like putting trust in princes—of no avail. I was looking over one or two leading agricul- tural papers the other day, and I was struck with the figures given of the value of imported food stuffs into England; midi thought a word or two on the subject would not be amiss. I may say that I have found, often, very often, great surprise expressed that the English people should import so very much that they could so easily produce :themselves. Take a conversation like the follow- ing::— .

'7Chickens," said a well-known West end poul- terer to a London reporter,' Why, bless you, they ,come from all parts of the world, every nation seems to be able to rear chickens except England. Here is a big cargo from Australia. Five shillings each, that's the price they fetch." "'Think of it, every nation except England can rear chickens, and sell them in London, noquestions asked and, at ss. each. This same London poulterer said, "I don't know what's the matter with our people; to think that, with all the depression they talk of, such a !market should be let go to outsiders as the poultry market; why, it seems to me disgraceful." "Why," le said, "it's the same in everything,from ducks to geese and turkeys, from chickens to every kind of .game and all sorts of birds." Another dealer said he hadn't had an English chicken 'in his place for days, and during the Derby and Ascot events he was selling "poultry at what -seemed fabulous rates:" This Londoner said " the English farmers were a silly prejudiced lot who had been doing one lkind of trade,aud went on sticking to it,even when it's left them, instead of taking up other branches that would be profitable." Taking up an Australian paper I•find reference madeto a special wharf that has been constructed in the Manchester dock for the fruit trade, and in connection therewith the Corporation opened a commodious sale room in Campfield 'market ground. The importation of apples into Manchester is •estimated at moo tons per annum, a large propor- tion of which come from Australia and Tasmania. 'Think of it. The competition that exists has forced the question of quality well to the front. All writers are agreed that it is of no use sending any- thing to the London market unless it is of first-rate quality. If this point is adhered to first-rate prices can be had. Australia last year exported 12,000 tons of butter. Six thousand tons of this received the Government brand as being first rate. Even then it did not command the highest prices. Denmark enjoys the reputation of mak- ing the best, and most uniform in quality, and her butter commands the highest prices. Denmark has adopted the principle of Pasteurisa- tion with excellent results. By the destruction of noxious germs and the cultivation of ferments which impart to the butter a special flavour and a keeping capacity, the quality of the product has been largely enhanced. I see nothing in all this but what an English farmer should understand and put in practice. The Government fruit expert of Vietoria writing on the past and present of fruit growing in that coloay says;—" We have now to look to Europe to take our surplus fruit, and I maintain that for a regular supply of a really good article the demand is almost unlimited in Great Britain alone. Vic- toria will send 10,000 cases of fruit to England this year. and is preparing to send tens of thousands in the future." What says the British farmer? La 1857 the area of land under fruit tree cultivation was 1939 acres. In 1893 it had increased to 31,370 acres, and in 1894 to 42,463 acres. These are telling figures, and show what the English farmer could do ' if he really wished to get hold of a bit of English money. That it is imperative that the English farmer shall take an all round view of the situation the following figures only need be given. In my pre- vious letter on this subject I referred to Argentina. This comparatively new country is fast becoming one of the powerful factors in the worlds supply of wheat. Four years ago the amount exported amounted to only 1,508.800 quarters of wheat and 94,440 sacks of flour, besides 3,220,000 quarters of maize. In 1894 she sent out 7,400,000 quarters of wheat, 322,670 sacks of flour, and only 283,000 quarters of maize. The wheat crop of the world last year was estimated to be 321,300,000 quarters. It is estimated that the Argentine Republic has about 240,000,000 acres of land well adapted for wheat cultivation. The labor employed is cheap Italian. " The country life," says a recent writer, "is poor, mean, and joyless, and the people who come from the land of song and art seem to have no ideas of redeeming the depressing surroundings of their bare, ugly comfortless homes, or of forming communities for social advantages of any sort," and yet. " they are the only successful wheat farmers." This must be all very bitter reading for the English farmer. Clearly his oecupation,as a wheat grower is gone. The good there is in competition, will, before many years are over, make itself felt in more craniums than that of the crank.

By the way, one of my Warrinzton friends, par- ticularly wishes me to keep Socialism out of one of my letters. I have tried to do so in this. 2409 Mission-street, an Francisco, Nov. 12, 1895.

AN INTERESTING CHAPTER OF LOCAL HISTORY.

BALTON CASTLE.

" Ff alton Castle and its Associations" was the subject of a lecture given by Mr. Joseph Pritchard in Runcorn , Technical Institute on Friday evening. There was a ! large attendanee,and the proceedings were presided over by Mr. William Handley, Chairman of the Free Library ! Committee, under whose auspices the lectures are held. A letter of /typology for the non-attendance was read from Sir John T. Brunner, Bart., M.?. In introdacing the lecturer, the Chairman said that they were met that night to improve their acquaintance I with the locsA history of their own district. He was 1 afraid !seal history was too often neglected. Many of them knew more of the history of places far distant than of those lying as their .very doors. For several years past the Library Committee had been gathering all the basks they could get relating to the local history of Runcorn and district, and they bad now got a fair number of nooks on that subject. and the librarian would be very glad to assist anyone in making their acquaintance with the books there were on the shelves. At the outset of his lecture,Mr. Tritchard said among the nanny interesting volumes with which their library abounded, there were few which to him appeared more deeply interesting thaa those which related to the histcrry of their native town and count. They had in the r immediate neighbourhood, ninny very important historical lauchnarks,but few whose history codicil be more distinctly traced than that of the ancient cattle of Halton. The 'first great fortresses built by Ethelfieda in Mercia were those of Tam- worth and Stafford, which were completed in the year 913, and she then turned her attention to the de- fiences a Cheshire. She built and extended the walls of .Chester and so protected the entrance to the Dee. Then -ehe built a strong castle or fortress at Runcorn, and so commanded the passage of the Mersey at its narrowest point. Anyone wbo remembered the rocky headlands which jutted into the river just where the Runcorn bridge now stood would be at once struck with the im- portance of such., strategical position, especially when he remembered that thewessels of war of those days were propelled by the power of the oar. She also founded a priory in Runcorn which stood very near to the place occupied by the Parish Church. Another fort she built at Latchford and so commanded the ford at Warrington. Anothenshe built at Thelwall—her place of residence, and anotherat Warburton, oppisite what was now known as Hollins Ferry or Hollins Green. In the rear of them all, as a last place of resort in case of defeat, she erected a much stronger fortress on Eddis- bury Hill, the highest point of Delainere Forrest, which is about 600ft..above the sea level, and commands a distinct view of theestuaries of the Mersey and the Dee, and also commanded the old Roman road from Chester to Manchester. The lecturer proceeded to deal with the conquest of the cmintry by William the Conquerer and his appointment of his nephew, Hugh Lupus, to the governor of Chester Castle, with the title of Earl of Ches- ter. This Earldom was founded in the year 1070, and existed as an independent power for nearly two hundred , years. Lupus' appointed his cousin, Nigel, to the )BARONY OF HALTON who built the castle on the hill from which he commanded a view of his possessions. For the better protection of his barony be placed his brethren in the neighbourhood . immediately surrounding the castle. To Huddard he • gave Aston and Sutton, and from Huddard, the great ' family of the iDuttons of Cheshire had sprung. To Edarus be gave a certain portion of land at Weston which had previously belom;sect to William de Weston, the head of an old Saxon family. To Wolmerus and Horswain be gave certain lands in Runcorn and Norton, while to WOlfaith who was his oldest brother, and a , priest, he gave the Parish Church of Runcorn.

of the castle was not a long one for he had to pay the debt of nature early. He was succeeded by his son, William Fitz Nigel, who at the time of the Doomsday survey is described as the owner of the broad lands of the barony of Halton which he held for a period of nearly Rfq years. 'William Fitz William was the third baron, and founded the church and abbey of Norton and removed the.priots who had been established at Runcorn for over 2.00 years, to Norton. This was the home of the priors until the reign of King Henry viii. (1536), when the king despoiled most of the monasteries of England. The third baron died without issue, and the honour of Halton was divided between his two sisters, Agnes and Maud. The husband of Agnes, Fitz Roger, became the sth baron of 'Halton. .JOHN OF GAIINT

_ Edwardllll. Gaunt made Halton Castle his occa- sional hunting seat and was said to have given the name to Fiddler's Ferry, for on one occasion when he had crossed the ferry (which was then known as Cuerdley Ferry) forthe purpose of examining the remainsof some old Danishiorts which were on the Lancashire side of the ferry.„ihe was detained on the river and entertained by a large !party of strolling fiddlers, and from this cir• cumsta,oce, he nicknamed it Fiddler's Ferry. In 1373 he he appointed Matthew de Rixton to Halton, and com- manded him to deliver to the prior and convent of Norton the tithes of the herbage and underwood in all the parishes within the Lordship of Halton in the same manner .as the predecessors had done. John died in February, 1398, and was succeeded by his son, Henry Bolingbroke, who became the fifteenth and

===== 0002601_18951207_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
LAST BARON OF HALTON.

On the dethronement of Richard 11., Henry Bolingbroke was crowned Xing of England, and all his lesser title be- coming estinct, the barony of Halton was no more. But while the barony was extinct, the castle still remained, and was etill of sufficient importance to be mentioned in the King's last will made in 1417, wherein be directed that his castle at Halton should after his decease pass to his brother, John, Duke of Bedford. After this it was plain that the old castle had lost its glory for after being a resideme for its barons for a period of over 300 years, it now

===== 0002601_18951207_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
BECAME ONLY A PRISON.

Among the records of Halton, there was one deserving of notice as it related to Runcorn. It was dated April 1481 wherein King Edward IV. ordered all vessels which should come to any port in theshire, first to call at Chester and discharge their cargo, even if they had to be reloaded, so that Chester might receive the benefit of the shipping fees, but one Edward Walshe, of Runcorn, held a special license as that he could sail his ship ta and from Runcorn without calling at the port of Chester. After alluding to the uses the castle was put to as a prison, the lecturer proceeded to speak of

ITS 1111P0UTANCE during the civil war of England. In 1642, when the sword of the great civil war was first drawn, John Savage, of Rocksavage, was seneschal of Halton Castle, and placed therein a sufficient garrison to hold it for the king, and the royal colours now floated proudly over the old walls of Halton. But while Halton, with the owner of Rocksavage at its head hell out for the king, the head of the trusts of Norton sided with the Parliamentarians and Colonel Henry Brooke was appointed high sheriff of Cheshire. Norton Priory was put into a defensive state, and on the 26th February, 1643, a small force of horse and foot with one cannon or field piece, appeared before the house and demanded its immediate surrender. Colonel Brooke refused to surrender, and succeeded in defeating the royal forces who ultimately retired to Halton Castle. The tide .of battle was now running strongly with the Parliamentarians, and after having captured Warrington on the 28th May, 1643, their victorious army made its way to Halton Castle and demanded its surrender. The castle was under the command of Captain Walter Primrose and he re- fused. A. siege was made and while it continued the Castle chapel which stood outside,was demolished. The siege continued for several weeks,and the besieged find- ing their provisions running shot, began to parley for terms of surrender, and on the 22,id July the castle was surrendered on honourable terms, and Sir William Brere- ton took possession in the name of the Parliament. Early in the following year (March 1644) the tide of battle again turned, and the castle was re-taken by Prince Rupert, but in July of the same year the castle once again submitted to the Parliamentarians and the Royal flag was once more hauled down, never to be raised again while it remained a fortress. On the 9th July a

COUNCIL OF WAR assembled at Warrington at whick there were present— Colonel Henry Brooke, Sir William Brereton, Sir George Booth, Sir Thomas Stanley, Roger Wilbraham, Henry Delves, Philip Mainwaring. and Robert Dutton, when an order was issued for the dismantling of Flalton, Rock- savage, Beeston, and several other Cheshire castles which have never been rebuilt. The office of constable and seneschal of Halton was conferred upon Colonel Henry Brooke and during his term of office the honour, fee, and manor, and castle of Haltail were

PUT UP FOR SALE and purchased by him. But his possession of it was not for long, for, being dissatisfied with the government of the country after the death of Cromwell, he in 1659 joined the Royalists,and was defeated and taken prisoner to Chester Castle, while his brother was expelled from the House of Commons and committed to the Tower for high treason. On the recall of the king in 1660, there was an opinion that the Brooke and Booth rising had only been a little too soon. To the honour of the Brooke family, though they had suffered greatly by the war, they were among the first contributors towards the erec- tion cf a new church in the place of the one destroyed during the war. That church remained to within the last fifty years when it was replaced b§ the beautiful structure now on the hill, and commanding the admir- ation of all who beheld it.

The lecturer was thanked for his ssrviceg on the motion of Mr. F. J. Norman, seconded by Mr. Cunning- ham.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FoorsALL.

When the news was telegraphed to Warrington on Saturday night that the first team had wo.i at Tyldesley by two goals to a try, there was a tremendous amount of astonishment, and a Peneral opinion that there must be some mistake. Had the result been the other way about, people would not have been surprised. Warring- ton had had such a run of ill-luck of late, that Tyldesley with their strong set of forwards were the last team that was expected to give Warrington their initial vic- tory away from home. Now that the Warringtonians have managed to break the spelt of ill-luck, let us hope that they will continue their victorious career, although they have a very big order to-day at Manningham.

Warrington not only won on Saturday, but they beat Tyldeslev at almost every point. Even forward, they held their own, and Burton and Houghton who played half were quite as good as the Berrys. This being the case, the Warrington threequarters for once got a chance which they were not slow to take advantage of, the result being that the wirepullers obtained the highest score they have made this season their previous highest score being seven points against Wakefield Trinity.

O'Brien signalised his re-appearance In the first team by scoring the first try which Warrington have secured since October 28. In fact Warrington had almost passed the whole of the month of November without a single score, and therefcre O'Brien is to be congratulated upon re-opening the scoring account. Barber also scored his second try of the season, thus sharing with Broady, Foden and Wilcock the credit of scoring twice. Goal kicking has been " off " completely previous to Satur- day, but then strange to say both tries, one of which was scored at the corner, were converted by Bur- ton, who has now kicked three.

The Tyldesley team have been beaten three times on their own ground this season, but Saturday's defeat was the heaviest they have bad either at home or away. Besides Warrington, Runcorn have beaten them on their own ground by a penalty goal, and Bradford by superior goal kicking. Their away defeats have been at the hands of Leigh by a try, Manningham by two tries, and Runcorn by two tries, so that Warrington's perform- ance has been by fair the best. There are many examples to be obtained of the vagaries of football, but a line drawn between Tvldesley and Warrington would take some beating for strange results. For example,although Tyldesley beat Batley by 16 points to 3, and Batley beat Warrington by 4 points to nil, yet Warrington beat Tyldeslev by IA points to 3. Again Tyldesley beat Old- ham by 11 points to 3, and Oldham beat Warrington by 6 points to nil, and yet Warrington beat Tyldesley as stated. These are great puzzlm of form, and we must not rush to the conclusion that because Tyldesley have beaten Manningham once, Warrington will do likewise. At the same time let us hope that they will do.

Warrington will be very short handed to-day in con- sequence of the absence of Barber and Nevins, and probably one or two other members of the team. Barber and Nevins are of course chosen to assist Lancashire and Yorkshire, and if they come off as well as they did against Cheshire, they will do well indeed. Burton is on the reserve, and may be called upon, and in that case, the Warrington three-quarter line will be very much weakened. The team which is chosen against Manning- ham to-day contains a dark horse for half-back, and is as follows :—Heesom, full back ; Burton, O'Brien ,Cross, and Broady,three-quarters :; Houghton and A. N. Other, half-backs; Burrows, Taylor, Swift, Hill, Andrews, Thornton, Wilco*, and Barrett, forwards, with Scott, Clark, and Fairhurst, reserves.

The various matches on Saturday contained several surprises besides the victory of Warrington over Tyldes- ley. Oldham on their own ground for instance only got the better of Rochdale Hornets by 13 points to eight,and were behind during a good portion of the game. Wid- nes hardly expected to came back victorious from Stock- port, after Runcorn failing to do better than draw there, the previous Saturday tut nevertheless they won by a goal to nil, and Stockport have the distinction or other- wise of only winning one home match out of eight, al- though they have won two away out of seven.

Runcorn after their temporary falling away the pre- vious Raturday,came again with a rush and beat Brough- ton Rangers by 18 points to nil, and are equal in points with Halifax the leaders of the league. The latter also demonstrated their superiority over Batley by gaining a victory of exactly the same dimensions as Runcorn. Batley now join the unfortunates who have had 100 points scored against them.

Bradford have been doing very indifferently of late, but they managed to inflict a defeat of a goal to nil on Brighouse Rangers, and the latter are now deposed from the position of leaders. Liversedge began the season very disastrously being nil-led in their first three matches, but on September Nth they beat Hunslet, and had not suf. fered defeat from that time until Saturday, when Leeds defeated them by a try to nil. They appear to be short of good reserves and this was one cause of their defeat on Saturday. Hull succumbed to the team who will meet Warrington to-day and thus Maaningham has suc- ceeded where a considerable number of good teams have failed.

A feature of the match es on Saturday was the fact that in the three fixtures between Lancashire and York- shire clubs which came off.that day,the Lancashire clubs were all victorious. Wigan beat Runslet, and St. Helens beat Huddersfield by exactly the same score of 11 points to three, while Leigh beat Wakefield Trinity by a goal to ail.

Though ill-luck has been so continuously attendant on the Warrington first team up to Saturday, it has been far otherwise with the A " team. Almost every week they have won and with some substantial majorities too. Saturday's match at home, with the Tyldesley "A" team proved no exception to the rule, for a handsome victory of 19 points to nil was the result. The visitors had the name of a very good. " A" team, therefore, it was with some surprise that the Warrington spectators witnessed the poor show they made.

In the home team there was some noteable changes, and judging from the play throughout the match, ad- vantageous ones. Spruce, from the Castle Rubber Works team, was full back, and is a smart one from all appearances, though he was but seldom called upon on Saturday. la the threequarter rank was a new player named Scott,who in addition to scoring,showed excellent judgment and dexterity throughout the match : in fact he gave the impression of being a player with good pos- sibilities before him. The remaining players had all taken part in the" A" team matches before and showed ”ice combination. All the backs worked splendidly and several bouts of passing won for them cheers in addition, to a try or two. With the victory of Saturday the "A' team have scored over 100 points,the exact record being 102 points for, and 42 against.

The visiting team having arrived late a match of 50 minutes was played, the first half under the rule forbid- ding the half backs from passing the last man in the Scrimmage. This trial gave very favourable results, for the game was remarkably open and fast, lessening the numbei of scrimmages and adding considerably to the pleasure of the spectators. With the new code of rules to come into force in January, Rugby matches will be immeasurably improved. Heavy forwards will be no longer necessary, speed and passing capabilities will be invaluable, nnd dropped goals may be expected to be the easiest points to obtain in the match.

"THE WORLD'S MARRIAGE

ASSOCIATION."

• The charges of fraud against the conductors of the World's Great Marriage Association, Limited, were further investigated at Bow-street Police Court on Mon- day. The Magiztrates having inquired whether it was alleged that the whole of the business of the Association was fraudulent, counsel for the prosecution said be could only speak at present with regard to the cases already before the court. The greatest difficulty they bad to contend with was getting evidence, many ptirsons, though admitting that they had been defrauded,refuslag to come forward. The Magistrate said he had received a number of letters from persons who had been married trough the Association. Several persons were called who gave evidence similar in character to that of the previous witnesses. A Northanspton shoemaker admitted under cross-examination that it was after receiving his portrait that one of the women with whom he ss as put in communication ceased to write to him, and that it was the police raid on the defendants' premises that interfered with his correspondence with another. The case was again adjourned.
