INTERESTING FACTS ABOUT A GREAT RAILWAY.

Which is the greatest corporation on earth ? Accord- ing to Mr. W. J. Gordon, it is the London and North- Western Railway. This compaay has a capital of £119,000,000, and a revenue of over £1,300 an hour; its 2,300 engines travel over 41,000,000 miles in the year ; it employs over 60,000 men, and carries over 156,00 C passengers a day. It makes everything it can for its own use, not only building its own bridges, engines, and rolling its own rails, but making the carriages and waggons, coal scuttles for its stations, and the wooden' hubs for the injured of its staff. To keep the line in a proper state of repair takes £2,500 a day. The approach roads, the bridges and signals, and other necessaries require £140.000 a year to keep them in working order. and about £lOO,OOO a year goes in painting and repair- ing, besides £30,000 in rebuilding. Even the wages bill for the permanent way alone amounts to £26,000 a month. The repairs to the rolling stock are of course a serious item. To keep it up to the mark takes about £300,000 a year, of which £llO,OOO goes in wages. Railway carriages are not built for nothing, a third-class one is worth £6OO, and a first-class composite is worth £2OO more, of which half is spent below the floor. The labour item in these days of machinery is very small, for it averages barely £2O a vehicle, notwithstanding that a first-class carriage takes 16 coats of paint before it is fit for service. It would be a good thing if some othet companies we know would imitate the London and North-Western—even in the matter of paint !—" West- minster Gazette."

===== 0002601_18951207_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
DE iall.

Verses suggested by the accidental death of Percy Gandy, youngest son of a widely-known and respected local townsman.

0 what a fragile thing is life, A span at most cf some few years ; A dream that fades in calm or strife, And dies in sunshine or in tears ! Childhood and youth, and bending age, Alike obey death's solemn call : And when they quit this earthly stage The open grave receives them all. What anguished thoughts our bosoms swell, What blinding tears will pity move, When in its dark and narrow cell We lay some dear one that we love. How vain are human comforts then To soothe the heart bereaved and lone! And vainer still the dreams of men To solve death's problem still unknown ! They say the dead are all alive. (A contradiction surely this :) That some in endless woe survive, While others share eternal bliss. But who from heaven e'er came to tell That mortal men in inhabit there: Has ever soul emerged from hell To voice its anguish and despair? No, no; the dead are in the grave Their final goal on nature's breast: Poor mariners tossed on life's rough wave, Their haven is eternal rest. There life and all its troubles cease, The brief and fevered dream is over: And there, in everlasting peace, The dead seem laid to rise no more. What lies beyond their dread abode We know not, and may never know; The secret still is hid with God, To whom our life and all we owe. 0 then be ours, in lowly trust, To wait the workings of His will: Assured that all our kindred dust Is compass'd with His mercy still! J. H. W. CLINDINNING.

SHIP CANAL

THE PORT OF MANCHESTER: LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC. __

_ During the seven days ended Friday, November 29,93 seagoing vessels, with an aggregate net registered tonn- age of 32,488 tons, arrived at and sailed from the vari- ous docks, wharves, kc., on the Ship Canal, as against 139 vessels of 40,088 tons during the previous week. The coastwise trade was represented by 82 vessels of 11,974 tons, agoinst 100 vessels of 18,343 tons, and foreign traffic by 31 vessels of 20,514 tons, against 39 vessels of 21,743 tons. Fifteen general cargoes were imported, seven of wood-pulp timber, ikc., from the Baltic, six of slate, stone, kc., three of green fruit from Spain, three of pig-iron, and one of iron ore. Amongst the exports were twenty-one cargoes of general merchandise, four of coal, two of salt, and one of pitch. At the present time in addition to the numerous regular liners, there are bound and fixed for Manchester from foreign ports ten steamers with green fruit, seven with wood-pulp, five sailing ships with logwood, four steamers with cotton. four sailing ships, and two steamers with tin-iber, two steamers with iron ore, a steamer with bauxite, and a barque with fustic.

===== 0002601_18951207_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
THE AUSTRALIAN TRADE.

The Government of Victoria has deputed Mr. James Sinclair, who recently visited the United States on behalf of the Minister of Acriculture, to represent the depart- ment in England this winter. He will make inquiries into the condition in which Australian butter, poultry, and rabbits have been landed in London by the mail steamers and the manner in which they are placed on the market, and will report on the arrangements pre- vailing in the Danish butter trade. He is also to investi- gate the conditions of the meat trade, and to make ar- rangements for the disposal of a largo crop of fruit— principally pears and apples—which will come into mar- kets from large plantations which are " coming into bearing" next teason. Mr. Sinclair will, according to the " Melbourne Argus," visit Manchester and other large centres of population with a view of opening up direct shipments.

A PILOTS' HOUSE AT EASTHAM LOCKS. By the joint action of the. Ship Canal authorities and the Mersey Thcks and Harbour Board a room in the Customs' building at Eastham has been set apart and furnished for the accommodation of sea-pilots awaiting the arrival of outward-bound vessels from Manchester. Ships coming down the canal in charge of Upper Mersey or Ship Canal pilots are boarded by the Liverpool bar pilots at Eastham locks, and it , has not unfrequently happened that these men have been kept waiting all night for vessels delayed in the canal. The action of the authorities in providing a comfortable shel- ter will, therefore, be keenly appreciated, particularly in tho winter months.

COLLISIONS AT RUNCORN AND SALTPORT•

On Friday morning last, a collision eccurred on the Ship Canal near the Brigdewater Leek, Runcorn. The steamer Ask, proceeding to Manchester, was trying to keep out of the way of a steamer coming in the opposite direction when she ran into the flat Fanny, alongside the canal wall. The force of the impact was such that the flat's starboard side was considerably damaged. In the afternoon of the same day.the steamer Faedrelandet, when passing Saltport on her way to Manchester, callided with two timber barges lying alongside a Nor- wegian barque. One of them belonged to the Ship Canal and the other to Messrs. Bate of R ancorn. Both vessels were seriously damaged.

A. LANDL lltD AND HIS TENANT.

CLAIM FOR RENT.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, William Rostock, builder, Brockton Heath, sued J no Thomas Rowlands Bewsey-street superi itendent of the Warrington district of the Prudential Assurance Company, for £4 7s. 6d. in lieu of noti. e to leave a house belonging to the plaintiff, in Stockton Heath. Mr. Arthur Browne appeared on behalf of the plaintiff, and Mr. Percy Davies for the defendant. Mr. Browne said that Mr. Rowlands Came to be the superintendent of the prudent' 1 Assurance Company in Warrington, on Januar% 4.11. 1895, and on that date, he came to Mr. B,stock inquirint about a house. He was shown through a house itiiewn as Sunny-side in Stockton Heath, and Mr. Rostock asked £25 a year for it. Mr. Rowlands said this was too much and offered £2O, saying that he would be is Warrington for three years. Mr. Rostock said he would cc nsult his son, who managed the property and pr milked to let Mr. R ierlamis known very soon. The next mornine. Mr. Rs wlands who at that time lived at Shrewsbury, sent a letter saying he would take the heuse at a yearly ren'al of £2O, and asked for the key. Mr. Rowlands afterwards c.me into the house, and on April 15th, he cone to pas. £5 for a Quarter's rent, at the same time giving a quarter's notice to leave. Mr. Rostock refused to take the notice as he said that the house was taken on a yearly rental. Mr. Rowlands kept in the house under protest until July 15th, when he paid his quarter's rent, and then gave a six month'S notice to quit which would terminate in January 1896. Mr. Rowlands at that time contended that only three month's notice was requisite, and only gave the six months notice under pretest. William Rostock, the plaintiff, corroborated the state- ment of his solicitors. He addei that defendant com- plained that the other persons in the houses adjoining only paid £l9 10s. per annum, and witness, therefore, agreed to allow the defendant to have the house on those terms. On the following July the defendant gave a six months' notice.—Cross-examined : Defendant never said the house was too small for his family, and that he only took the house temporarily. The house waS not taken at three months' notice and three months' rent. No one ever complained that witness had stolen a tempor- ary receipt which showed that the house was subject to a quarter's notice. John Rostock said that he manage -1 his father's pro- perty. He corroborated his fathers' statement.—Cross examined He never agreed that there was to be a quarterly tenancy of the house on any occasion. He did not tear up a temporary receipt which had been given to Mr. Rowland, . Miss Rowlands burnt that. Fanny Rostock said that when the defendant brought the first rent, she gave him a temporary receipt. He also gave a quarter's notice, which her father objected to as defendant was a yearly tenant. _ .

Annie Bostock iave.simikr evidence. John Thomas Rowlands, Bewsey-st., said that in Jan- uary of this year, he was appointed Superintendent of the Prudential Assurance Company, Warrington district. When he took the house belonging to Mr. postock, a quarter's rent and a quarter's notice was agreed upon. He denied that any receipt was ever burnt by his daughter. Further evidence having been given, Mr. Davies contended that the terms were agreed upon. for si quarter's notice. His Honour, however, held that the evidence showed that the house was taken on a yearly tenancy, and he gave judgment for the plaintiff.

Jr is now generally expected that Subsidising the Landlords. one of the first attempts at legis- lation by the present Govern- ment will be in the direction of relieving the landed interest "—in other words, the landlords. 'This relief will, it is believed, be given in the shape of an imperial grant in aid of local rates from the large surplus which it is probable the CHANCELLOR of the ExcneouEß will have in his possessical at the end of the fieancial year. This is the Tory way of attempting to cure the agricul- tural depression which they say is mostly due to foreign competition. That it is an entirely wrong - will be the opinion of almost everybody who has given an unbiassed consideration to the land question. It will indeed be no cure at all, or at most a cure of the most partial and temporary character. It will certainly be a boon for the land- lords, as by relieving the rates of the tenant farmers it may for a time lessen the urgency of the cry for a reduction of rents. But from the point of view of the national welfare the landlords aro the last party which should be considered. They base their claims for imperial subsidies on the ground that the system of free imports is depre- ciating the value of their land, but they ; altogether ignore the fact that under Free Trade rents have been raised to an enormous extent. It was estimated by the late Sir JAMES CALEB, who was a very competent authority, that in the first 30 _years after.the introduction of Free Trade the "value of agricultural land in this country increased by no less an amount than 300,000,000. The , landlords have in the past had 1l the benefit of this immense growth of value. When rents were rapidly increasing it was not even whispered that I the State was entitled to any share of the unearned increment." Such a suggestion would, I have been in the opinion of such Tories as the MARQUIS of SALISBURY, an incitement to epoliation and robbery. Yet the Tories of to-day have accepted the doctrine that while landlords , may themselves enjoy all the increases of rent, they t• are entitled to State subsidies when rents begin A to decline. It is a monstrous doctrine, but mon- strous as it is, there is a concensus of opinion that it will be embodied in legislative proposals almost ILS soon as Parliament meets. We do not wish to deny that the agricultural industry -of Great Britain is far from being in the healthy end prosperous condition that it ought to be in considering the inexhaustible market for agricul- tural produce there is close at hand. The reason why it is not prosperous might well be the subject of an all-round and exhaustive inquiry . • But that inquiry would not be instituted by the Tory party, for the simple rea- ason that all such inquiries tend to bring out in • the clearest colours the fact that the decadence of agriculture is due to a land system which has been formed by the landlords in 'the past and is main- tained by the friends of landlords to-day. This N system has not been framed with a view to the : greatest good of the greatest number, but with the object of maintaining and strengthening a caste of landowners. The system is breaking down under • the strain of modern development and the corn- ' • petition of British colonies and other countries 1 whose agriculturists are not shackled and obstructed as those of England are. The projected subsidies will do little or nothing to counteract the forces which are gradually evolving a new and better land system, though they will put a few *millions, more or less, into the pockets of the 'landlords, and that, after all, seems to be the chief elm of the agrarian policy of the Tory party in England just as it was and is in heland.

Ma. JOHN- MORLEY made an in- Mr. Morley at teresting and useful speech on Newcastle. political questions in his old con- I.* , 4stituency of Newcastle on Monday. He is not one of those impetuous politicians who think that both the Liberal party and the programme of reforms to which it has set its hand were swept ~, out of existence at the last election. Nor does he 'think that the party has anything to recant. There is always a tendency after a defeat, politi- cal or otherwise, to find -a scapegoat, and there has been no departure from the rule in connection with the election of this year. In the opinion ot some the Local Veto Bill was the cause of the disaster; according to others disestablishment or ,some other measure which the Liberal Govern- ment had undertaken was the culpable cause.

But, after all, these arc rather bootless contro- versies. The Liberal party is the great instru- inent of progress in this country, and as such it is the medium through which progressive people seek to effect their objects. All the - questions which the late Government attempted to legislate upon were living questions which, 41a Mr. MORLEY said, the Liberal Govern

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

MEMORIAL SERVICES.

At Ellesmere•st. Chapel, on Sunday,memciial services were held, and sermons preached by the Rev. John Stafford, pastor. In the morning he spoke of the death of the Rev. Peter Mackenzie, who bad done a great and important work among the Wesleyan body, and in the evening he alluded to the late Mr. Rot 2rt Hoyle and Mr. Charles Johnson, both of whom died at Runcorn at the age of 73 years. A TICKET OF LEAVE MAN.

At Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday morning before Messrs. James Handley, P. Speakman and E. Imison, a boatman, named Joseph Yates, of no address, a ticket- of-leave man, was charged with failing to report him- self to the police and notifying his place of abode. The prisoner had bean arrested by the Manchester police the previous morning at eight o'clock. sergeant Gill proved the charge as to the prisoner not reporting himself. The magistrates committed him to gaol for seven days.

Loss OF A RUNCORN SCHOONER.—TIIREE LIVES LOST. Thomas Kinsman, mate of the Lancaster schooner Joseph, with coal trom Runcorn to Flushing, has ttle- graphed from London to his relations near Falmouth, than the schooner sank off the Lizird, and that Captain George Worth and an ordinary seaman, both belonging to Cornwall, and an Irish seaman were drowned. The Joseph was seen by two schooners beating astern of them round the Lizard on Sunday morning, and it was noticed that some of her sails were gone. It is supposed that Kinsman Wr9 rescued and conveyed to Lonuon by a passing steamer.

CAMDEN FOREIGN MISSIONARY ANNIVERSARY. As will be seen trom an auvertiseinent, the Wesleyan Foreign Missionary Anniversary at Camden Chapel will be held on Sunday. Sister Annie will conduct a serv:i in the afternoon, and in the evening, the Rev. T. Brackenbury will preach. On Tuesday, the annual meeting will be held when the chair will L taken by Mr. P. Speakman, J.P., C.O. An addr s will be de- livered by the Rev. A. W. Hall, late missionary on the West Coat of Africa, on the subject " Mission work in the land of death." A number of curioAties • will be exhibited, including an Arab and Fantee dress. Mr. J. Robinson will give aa exhibition of slides illustrative oh Foreign Missionary work.

NrtRINERS' CHURCH, RUNCORN. In connection with this church an entertainment was given on Monday evening, and was largely attended. Mr. Matthew Sweetman occupied the chair and delivered a brief address. The programme was of a most interest- ing character, and included tongs by Mr. Stansley. of Widnes, Miss Maggie Hill, Miss A. Dunning, Miss Nellie Fenwick and Mr. Williamson. Mr. Hulse and Miss Turner recited, and Mr. Thomas Sweetinan gave a read- ing. Mr. Gardner performed an organ voluntary. An interesting item was the piccolo and organ duet entitled "Gloria," by Mr. Mack, and Mr. Crossley. The usual vote of thanks brought an enjoyable evening's entertain- ment to a close. THE THEFT OF A COAT AT RUNCORN.

j Elizabeth Ann Martin, a girl residing at Runcorn, was brought before the magistrates at the jßuncorn Police Court, on Wednesday morning, charged on remand with stealing a coat value 10s. ed. from the !front of the shop of Mr. Lightfoot, High-street,Runcorn, and also with doing damage in the police cell by break- ing the windows. The evidence was published in our last issue. She was then remanded to linutsford for a week in order that some of her parents might be present. j Her father now appeared, and the magistrates bound the girl over to come up for judgment when called upon. LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD INQUIRY AT RUNCORN. Mr. W. J. F. Clerke, C.E., an inspector of the Local Government Board held an inquiry at Runcorn Town Hall on Tuesday morning with respect to the applica- tion of the Urban District Council for sanction to borrow £660 for the purpose of water supply. There were pre- I sent Messrs. James Pritchard and John Wilson (chair- man and vice-chairman of the Water Committee), F. A. Lake (law clerk),E. Marshall (secretary), M. Barker and James Wilding (surveyors). It was explained that the loan was required for the extension of mains in Norman- road and Heath-road, for the purchase of several Deacon Waste Water detection meters,an indicator for the reser- voir, &c. The proceedings were of a formal nature and the inspector having paid a visit to the reservoir, promised that he would report in due course. PROPOSED TESTIMONIAL TO SUPERINTENDENT ATHERTON.

As will be seen from an advertisement in another column, it is proposed ta present Superintendent Ather- ton with a testimonial on his retirement from the 1 Cheshire County Constabulary. It is felt that Superin- tendent Athertcn's long and meritorious career deserves recognition, not only from the inhabitants of Runcorn, which is his native town, but also from the people of the wider area over which he has had charge during the last years of his active and efficient service. The testi- monial will be et a suitable character, and due intima- tion of its presentation will be given to subscribers. Mr. Francis Wiswall. C.E.,is chairman of the committee,and Mr. J. B. Rolland, manager of the Runcorn branch of Parr's and the Alliance Bank, Limited, has kindly con- sented to act as treasurer of the fund. The subscription list will close on the 14th ult.

THE LATE REV. PETER MACKENZIE.

The Rev. W. George preaching in St. Paul's Wesleyan Chapel on Sunday to young men, in the course of his sermon referred to the loss sustained by the Methodist Church, by the death of the Rev. Pater Mackenzie. He stated that no one was more generally beloved from the Dan to the Beersheba of Methodism, and that those who knew him most intimately would remember him with the greatest affection. Behind that broad and trenchant humour, which made his personality so attractive to the crowd, there beat a heart as gentle, as genial, as gener- ous, as loyal as in any man he ever knew. He was a unique man. Methodism had only bad one Peter Mackenzie. At one time it was thought that humour was the gift of the evil one, but Peter Mackenzie, like Spurgeon and others, had shown that it might be used for the highest and best of purposes. When he (Mr. George) heard that be was gone he felt as King David did when be knew that Abner. the sou of Ner was dead, and said "Know ye not that there is a Prince and a great man fallen this day in Israel." RURIDRCANAL CONFERENCE AT RUNCORN. _ . _ .

• At the Seamen's Institute, Runcorn, -t:;i'id—oTiday after- noon, the annual conference in connection with the Rural Deanery, of Frodsham was held. The Rev. Canon Wood, rural dean,presided. SEAMEN'S MISSION, RIINCOIN.

In connection with this mission,the first of a series of P.S.A. services was held on Sunday afternoon. 'rho at- tendance was most encouraging, and the service throughout was a complete success. Brief and appro- priate addresses were delivered by Mr. Shaw,the energe- tic missionary in connection with the Seamen's Mission, and Mr. Cooke. The musical portion of the service was specially attractive, solos being effectively rendered by Miss Coward, Miss Jones, Mr. Cross, and Mr. Moores.— The usual entertainment was held en Tuesday evening, when there was again a crowded attendance. Mr. Shaw presided, and addressed a few words of welcome to the many sailors present. The programme was of a miscel- laneous character, and included songs by Mr.C. Aekers, Mr. W.Ackerley, Mrs. Harrison, Miss N. Douglas, Mr. Bird, Mr. Price, Mr. Hailwood, Mr. T. Wood, and Mr. Kennedy. A pleasing item was the piccolo solo by Mr. Mack. Mr. Lawton contributed two mouth organ solos in a masterly manner. Glees were given by the Misses Temple and Mr. Temple,and Messrs.Noster,Wood,Moore, Mack, Temple, and Crossley. A re,iding by Mr. Cooke was much appreciated. A vote of thanks to those who had contributed to the programme having been passed, an enjoyable evening's entertainment concluded. ASSEMBLY BALL AT RUNCORN.

the-i

The fifth assembly ball was held in the Public Hall, Runcorn. on Wednesday evening, and was most success- ful in every respect. At 8.30 when dancing commenced there was a nice company present. The hall had been tastefully decorated for the occasion by Mr. Steele, a fine effect bzing produced by the artistic arrangement of flags, shields, bannerettes and bunting. The stretcher which had been laid was much appreciated by the dancers. Messrs. G. Shone and T. Kirkham were assisted in their honorary duties of Masters of Cere- monies, by Messrs. Clement Handley, and T. Sherratt. Mr. Merchant's fine quadrille band tram Chester was in attendance. No fewer than twenty-two dance s were merrily engaged in, the shadow dances being an inter- esting feature of the evening's programme. Messrs. William Lea and Son, of High-street, were responsible for the lime-light effects, and they are to be congratu- lated upon the admirable manner they carried out the woik. A refreshment beet had been erected in the ante-room by Mr. Charles Dutton, of Wilson's Hotel. well-stocked with wines, and other drinks as well as many other appetising morsels. The arrangements for the ball were carried out by a committee el which Mr. Gilbert R. Pritchard was secretary, and Mr. Clement Handley, treasurer.

MYSTERIOUS DEATH OF A BOATMAN.

Adam Peacock, employed on the canal boats Ada and Una, belonging to Messrs. Simpson, Davies and Son, of Runcorn, was found dead on the cabin floor, about six o'clock on Friday morning. The boats were lying oppl- site Highf eld Tannery, Runcorn, and Peacock retired to bed apparently in his usual health. About 1 a.m. he was heard poking the cabin fire, which was of coke, but when John Jacksen went to rouse him at 6 o'clock he was found lying on the floor dead. Death was probably due to suffocation. Deceased was a married man and resided at Stonehills, Runcorn. SALE OF WORK AT rRINITY SCROOLROOM.

On Thursday afternoon a two days' sale of work was opened in Holy Trinity Schoolroom with the object of raising funds for the church missionary society,pastoral aid society, and parochial objects. It was the twelvth of its kind, and the opening ceremony was performed by Mrs. Moon, of Watford. There was a good attend- ance. The stalls had been nicely draped for the occa- sion, and were in charge of the following :—Principal stall, Mrs. Preston, assisted by the Misses Preston, and Mrs. Walter Timmins ; Mrs. Barker, assisted by the Misses Housefield superintended the plain %cork stall ; and a toy stall was in charge of Mrs. Bartram assisted by her two children, and Miss Merriam and Ivy Darby- shire. The refreshment stall was presided at by Mrs. Charles Timmins, Mrs. Meadowcroft, Mrs. Horsefield, Mrs. Tyrie, and Miss Matthews. Mrs. Moon having opened the bazaar with a few words, a vote of thanks was passed to her on the motion of Dr. Preston,seconded by Mr. Charles Timmins.

THE RUMOURED WIDENING OF RUNCORN BRIDGE.

The rumour which gained currency last week respect- ing the widening of Runcorn Bridge seems to have little foundation. The impression seems to have arisen from a paragraph in the daily papers on the private legisla- tion of 186, wherein it was sPt forth that the London and North-Western Bill contains clauses authorising the company to construct a branch railway in Leigh, and to widen their Grand Junction Railway at Warrington, Runcorn, and Little Sankey. The Bill contains the fol- lowing paragraph :—" The widening of the Company's Grand Junction Railway between a point 550 yards or thereabouts south west of Moore Station, and a point 55 yards or thereabouts north of the bridge which carries Liverpool-road, Warrington,over the railway, which said intending widening will be situated in the parishes, townships, and places following or some of them, that ie to say Runcorn,Reckwick, Moore, Acton Grange,Walton Inferior, and Little Sankey." It will be seen that Run- corn is only mentioned incidentally as being a place where the extension is likely to touch.

RUNCORN WESLEYAN CIRCUIT TEMPERANCE SOCIETY.

ANNUAL MEETING..

In St. Paul's Wesleyan Chapel on Monday eveninss,the annual temperance. meeting in connection with the Run- corn Wesleyan circuit was held. Mr. John Simpson occupied the chair, and he was supported on the plat- form by the Rev. T, Brackenbury, Rev. C. De Patron Gliddon, Rev. Sampson Weaver. temperance secretary to the Liverpool district ; Messrs. T. Simister. D. Bisbrown, A. R. Norman, John Robinson and Mr. J. D. Jones (local secretary). There was a fair attendance. After devo- tional exercises Mr. J. D. Jones in presenting the annual report for the Runcorn circuit stated that there were 456 members in the adult section, and in the Bands of Hope societies 1,322 members, being an increase of 62 in the adult section, and 15 in the bands of hope. The membership in each band of hope in the circuit was as follows : Brunswick 450, Mill Brow 261, Camden 180, Halton 155, Preston Brook 65, Weston 90, Weston Point 75, and Nelson-street mission 45. Steps had been taken to establish an adult section at each preaching place on the plan, and by next year they hoped to report that a flourishing temperance society was in active operation in all these places. Great things in this respect were anticipated from the formation of the Wesley Guild in connection with St. Paul's. The treasurer reported that the society was indebted to him to the amount of El 15s. 11d. The question of intemperance pressed as urgently as ever for settlement. Its appalling extent and dire results, which the Conference annually called upon them to contemplate, were as terrific as ever ; so that they date not relax their efforts in relation thereto. Could they do better in their .work than to act up to teaching of the old formula "total abstinence for the individual and prohibition for the State." How delighted every loyal and temperate subject of the Queen must have been when he read in the papers what she said to Khama and the Bechuana Chiefs at Windsor Castle little more than a week ago. The Queen said, "I approve of the provision excluding strong drink from COUT country. I feel strongly in this matter, and am glad to see that the Chiefs have determined to keep so great a curse from the people." (Applause.) The Chairman said in the course of his address that he thought it was quite true that everyone would admit that drink was a most expensive indulgence. During the last year taking every man, woman and chili, in this country, the amount Ppent on drink had been no less than £3 10s. 6d. a head. Of course there were comparatively few children that took drink, and there as a great number of parents who didn't take drink, so that the amount spent by those who did take intoxi- cants must be enormous. It was rather a striking fart that in 1894 the amount spent on wine was £14,000.000, and the amount spent on beer and spirits £125,000,000, so that those figures showed where the great bulk of the drinking took place. They believed that the drink curse was not only the great cause of poverty, but of disease, and crime. The churches were unanimous in their opinion that the great cause of crime in this country was drink. He was pleased to say that out of 158 students in the Wesleyan theological colleges 149 were total abstainers. (Applause.) Thus there were only nine out of 158 that were not total abstainers. He hoped before long all the students would he teetotalers. In conclusion, he urged all to use their influence to win , others to total abtinence. (Applause.) I The Rev. C. De Patron Gliddon alluded to the critic- ism passed upon total abstainers at the Brewers' Exhibi- tion, and proceeded to refer to the effects of drink on the children of the country. Out of 50.000 children medi- cally examined, It was found that thirty per cent, were suffering from mental or physical defects largely attri- buted directly or indirectly, to the drinking habits of their parents. That might be taken as an average of the whole. If it were only on account of the children he would le on the side of temperance. (Applause.) Let them take courage. Be was afraid some of them were disheartened because they thought they were re- buffed during the last election. He believed great good would come out of the rebuff. They have no cause to be disheartened. He never believed the cause of tem- perance would ever fail so long as God lived. (Applause.) The Rev. Sampson Weaver said he did not feel the least discouraged with the outlook for temperance. It did not depend on any political party triumph, it was not allied to any one political party. It did not follow that if one party had been beaten at the polls, the tem- perance movement was going back. The fact was, the temperance movement had reached the point where it now stood in spite of political parties, and until they heard of hundreds of thousands of teetotalers giving up, and their bands of hope being suspended, and their temperance societies broken up, he did not see any reason to be discouraged. (Applause.) A vote of thanks to the speakers and the deputation, was passed on the motion of Mr. D. I3isbrown, seconded by Mr. Robinson.

About twenty minutes to eleven on Thursday night a working man named George Bastard, living in apart- ments in Welden-street, Walton-read, committed suicide on the Woodside Ferry boat Mersey, while crossing from Birkenhead, by jumping overboard.

WESTON POINT.

-- SERIOUS BURNING CASE.—A child, nine years of age, named Mary Pesch, 28, Sandy-lane, Weston Point, was seriously burnt on Monday morning. It appears that she was taking the kettle from the fire when her clothes became ignited, and before the flames could be extin- guished she was burnt about the arms, face and chest. Dr. McLnnan was sent for, and he speedily arrived,and dressed the wounds. She is now progressing satisfactorily

The minutes of th-e—Scb-o-or •Attendance Committee stated that 69 parents were summoned,and 64 appeared. Proceedings were ordered to be taken against four par- ents. The visitors' reports for the month ending Novem- ber 22nd. were read. They showed a decrease in the attendance as compared with the corresponding month of last year as follows e—Number on the rolls, 3,440, against 3,465 ; average attendance 2,757 against 3,917, and per centage of attendance, 80 against 84. The fal- ling off was attributed to sickness amongst infants, and to the absence of many children from the new Board schools, owing to a difficulty about the school fees.—Dr. Gastaldi in moving the adoption of the minutes,said the parents summoned before the committee,were nearly the same each time for the offence of not sending their chil- dren regularly to school. He thonght it was requisite that there should be new powers to deal with them.—Mr. Taylor seconded.—Dr. McDougall wished to know if the exact number of those absent on account of the school fees' difficulty could be given.—The Chairman stated that there were about 100 children absent from Brunswick and Granville-st. schools for that reason.— Dr. McDougall: On whose authority was that state- ment made P—The Chairman : I have inquired on one or two occasions, and have been so intormed.—Dr. McDougall : Is there any authorative report on the sub- ject from the schools or the visitors P—The Clerk : The report from Mr. Crosby is that 65 were absent from his school the peevious day on that account. There would not be so many for the whole of November.—Dr. Mc Dougall : As a matter of fact the report is rather mis- leading.—The Clerk : In the report of the School At- tendance Committee no number is stated.—Dr. McDougall : Statements of such an impostant character should be founded upon fact and not upon hearsay.— The Clerk : The report is Co doubt founded upon facts. During October there were 43 absent,and for November 82. This he explained was in the tenys' sceoels. The fieures clearly snowed that there were 40 more absent in November than in October—Dr. AlcDoagall : Is it as a matter of fact, your opinion that the absence of the eo was entirely due to the fact that they had to ply feei ? —The Clerk: The visitors do riot report to that effect. There are two causes for the reduced attendances—one is sickness amongst children. That is a matter of fact— (laughter)—and the other cause is an objection to pay fees on the part of the parents, whose children had at- tended the Wesleyan schools.—Dr. McDougall : Does it apply to Granville-street schools as well ? The Clerk : There were 45 absentees in October , and 66 in November. Dr. McDougall said it did not appear that the average attendance in the infant's school- where no fees were charged, was better than in the boy's and girls' schools where they had to be paid. How did they account for that P—The Clerk informed the ,Board that the attendance in the Infants' school was affected by the sickness which the doctor most have known was prevalent.—Dr. McDougall That is merely a statement. It is a mistake to make statements unless based upon facts.—The Clerk again explained that the average attendance w-es 77 in the infants, and only 67 in the boys, and usually the atten- dance was very much worse in the infants.—Dr. McDou- gall said from his point of view he held a different opin- ion—Mr. Taylor : Let Us have your opinion and deal with the question.—Dr. McDougall : No, I may do so later.—Mr. Taylor While we are discussing this matter we may as well have it altogether.—Dr. McDougall.: It is no use makine statements unless they are reliable.— Mr. Taylor : I think all the secretary has got to do is to give in the facts. Ido not see that he is responsible. I can only say that at the last school attendance coin mittee a number of parents came before us with regard to one set of Board schools being free and the others not and gave as the reason why the children had not at- tended school that they objected to pay the fees. The question was dealt with on the agenda and therefore tnere was no cause for this discussion and the inquiries made by the doctor,and the time of the members should not be taken up in this manner.—Dr. McDougall said as a matter of fact he could not accept Mr. Taylor's mild statement because he should deal with the question later on for the benefit of the public notwithstanding Mr. Taylor's time, which no doubt was very valuable to himself (Mr. Taylor: Hear, hear.) When any individual accepted the responsibility to discharge public duties, for which there seemed to be an especial fondness in the minds of some people they ought to keep the public abreast of what the Board was doing, and the mere fact of bodily presence in the room was not discharging a public duty according to his view of the subject. Nay, he must go further, and say as a matter of fact, if the Board kept more abreast I with the public in those matters they would have less I trouble with the public than they bad, and be a great deal less in the hands of wirepuller s than they were.— The Rev. Dr. Gastaldi explained that many parents appeared before the committee who adduced as a reason for non-attendance that they had not money to pay the fees.—Mr. Taylor said the parent did not plead that they could not afford to pay. Many of the people came before the committee that could afford to pay—(Dr. McDougall: That is the point)—but they objected to paying fees in a Board school when another Board school was free.—The Chairman : That was the prin- cipal plea, and not on account of sickness ? Mr. Taylor : Yes.—The Minutes were then confirmed.—Dr.McDougall did not vote.

THE EDUCATION DEPARTMENT AND THE CHARGING OF FEES. _ . _

The School Management Committee's minutes origin- ally recommended that no'fees be charged to the scholars attending Brunswick-st. boys' school and Granville-et. girls' school on and after Monday, December 9th.—The Clerk also read a letter from the Education Department submitting a representation under section 5 of the Elementary Eduction Act of 1891,which their lordships had received for 67 parents of children of school age residing at the addresses given stating that they desired their children might receive tlieir educatian without payment of fees,and that in the northern division of the , school district of the borough of Runcorn there is an insufficient amount of public school accommodation without payment of fees. Their lordships asked for an answer to a number of questFons.—The Chairman moved the confirmation of the minutes.—Canon Wood proposed that the Board go into committee for the purpose of con- , sidering the matter.—The Rev.Dr.Gastaldi seconded tho proposition.—Dr.McDougall said the subject was an im- portant one. He did not wish to nnow any discredit upon the applicants, but therg did not appear to be any substantial evidence before erns Board that the indivi- duals were bona-fide.—The Clerk stated that the appli- cants had been verified.—Dr. McDougall said the ques- tion was,are the children of these people attending these schools P—The Chairman said he believed the Clerk was able to give those particuhas.—The Board then went into committee, and after a heated discussion extending over an hour, it was decided to recommend in substitu- tion of the recommendation of the School Management Committ.se the following :—" That the head teachers of Brunswick-st. and Granville-st. schools be instructed to grant free places to all scholars who make application I for them."—in moving the adoption of this minute, the Chairman said he thought it would meet the desire of those who had made application for free places.—Dr.. Gastaldi seconded, and it was carried flees. con.

ASSOCIATION OF SCHOOL BOARDS. The Rev. Canon Wood and Mr. E. Imison were ap- pointed to represent the Board at the annual meeting of the Association of S,hool Boards.

The House of Lwds on Thursday gave an important decision in regard to partnerships. A retiring partner, it was laid down, may do anything which an outsider might do, but he must not avail himself of his special knowledge of the old customers to regain without con- sideration that which he had parted with for value. At the Clerkenwell Police Court,on Thursday Madame Beason, the wife of a musical instrumen.; manufacturer, was charged under an extradition warrant with sterling money and sfnurities to the total value of £60,000, the property of her husband. The prosecutor and his wife, it was stated, were married in accordance with the French law, the marriage contract establishing a com- munity of goods between the husbansl and wife. The ! term of the contract on which the prosecution mainly rely to support the charge of stealing states that " whichever of the consorts has abstracted or concealed property belonging to the community forfeits his or her share." The prisoner left the prosecutor in 1894, and it is alleged that she subsequently absconded with securi- ties to the value of £60,000. The case was again ad- journed,

The others, such as the better government of Ireland, liquor reform,Welsh disestablishment, and registration have been post- poned but have not been an ann h Hated. There is still a strong demand for each of these reforms ; there is, as Mr. MORLEY said, "not one of them which is not at this moment quick and alive." And, he might have added, there is not one of them which the next Liberal Government will be either I willing or able to ignore. In dealing with foreign affairs Mr. MORLEY was, taking all the circum- stances into account, extremely lenient with Lord SALISBURY. He quoted Mr. BRIGHT'S saying that the present Prime Minister is a man of a good deal of" haughty unwisdom," but he added the opinion that Lord SALISBURY is not likely to do anything unwise in the present Eastern crisis. So far events seem to justify this opinion. Lord SALISBURY seems to have done extremely little that is wise or unwise. He has:certainly failed to I protect the Armenians in accordance with the pledges entered into at the time he was a party to the framing of the Ber- lin Treaty. His policy may be a wise and masterly one, but it is certainly one of "masterly inactivity." There is, undoubtedly,at the present moment a great deal of impatience, which may rapidly develope into a flame of indignation, with the part that England is playing in this Eastern crisis. There is additional evidence to hand almost every day to show that she is not protect- ing her Christian protegoes in Asia Minor from further outrages and massacres. And in addition to this the Sultan seems to flout Lord &Lisa cunt, and through him the country he represents, in the fine old Turkish way. Certain Turkish offi- cials who distinguished themselves by the cruelties they:practiced upon the Asiatic Christians were denounced by the British Ambassador at Constantinople, and for a time they appeared to be degraded from their offices, but now it is said the SULTAN is heaping honours upon them in a very ostentatious manner. This does not indi- cate that Lord SALISBURY is to be credited with any diplomatic victory at Constantinople, nor is of good auguty tor the immediate future of the Armenia

When it is said that the Ashanti expedition will consist of 1,500 troops only, it does appear to be rather a small affair, but then in addition to the fifteen hundred lighting men no fewer than 10,003 carriers will march to Coomassie. The peculiar ties of warfare in tropical Africa can be understood when it is said that the whole of these 10,00) porters will have to walk the greater part of the way in single file, and in fact it is estimated that the leaders will not be a great distance from Coomassie when the last man is leaving Cape Coast Castle. King Prempeh, the cause of all these war- like preparations, is gracefully described by one- correspondent as a " little beast, given to debau- chery but with fits of Dutch courage." It is said that he drinks a pint of whisky at a time from a bottle without:a pause, and then ask tor more.

Those who have taken the opportunity of hear- ing Professor Thoumaian, the Armenian exile, who has spoken at several of the places of worship in Wairington,have received an amount of information with regard to the Armenian atrocities which will enable them to realise all the more the terrible con- ditions under which the Armenians are placed by their Turkish tyrants. The Professor is a well educated man, and has a good command of the English language. He does not indulge in idle de- clamation,or florid language, but carries conviction to his hearers by his calm manner of expressing his opinions to his hearers. He says that so far from the reports of the massacres being exaggerated, they are rather understated. He also exhibited a num- ber of the instruments of torture used by the Turks who are, it is quite evident, capable of committing the mast appalling cruelties. The professor wa3 very strong in his denunciation of the indifference and supineness of the Great Powers, and also of the famous Berlin treaty with its "Peace with honour:, That treaty he contended was the cause of all the massacres which have since taken place, as but for it,Armenia would hate been freed from the Turkish yoke.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. McDougall in tile monthly report stated that the number of deaths registered during the past month amounted to thirty-two. Three were inquest cases, and of the remaining twenty-nine, eight were due to bron- chitis, two to plithisis, two to heart disease, four to natural decay, two to pneumonia, three to convulsions, two to premature birth, and one each to peritonitis, mesenteric disease, liver disease, bright's disease, hydro- cephalus, and one to inflammation of the lining mem- brane of the brain. The thirty-two deaths showed an average death rate of 2002,. and exclusive of the inquest cases of 18-03 of the estimated population tor the twelve months. Forty-two births were registered during the month. The report was adopted.

HIGHWAYS AND BUIL-DINGS COMMITTEE.

It was recommended by this committee that Mr. E. Marshall, secretary and accountant to the Rencorn Urban District Council, be appointed under section 13 of the Private Street Works Act, 1892, to collect arrears of principal and interest due in respect to the making of Bold-street, and that the seal of the Council be affixed to such appointment. —Thesz minutes wers approved on the motion of Mr. Taylor, seconded by Mr. Sweetman. HEALTH COMMITTEE. This committee recommended that the Medical Offi- cer of Health be instructed to furnish the Health Com- mittee with a report forthwith on the recent epidemic of measles as requested tiy the Local Governrneet Board. The committee reported that they had received a letter from the Runcorn Rural District Council asking if the Council would be prepared to join with them in previd- ing sufficient hospital accommodation for infectious diseases for the two districts. The committec recom- mended that a reply be sent to the effect that the Coun- cil have sufficient accommodation for the treatment of cases of infectious diseases in their district and are not disposed to Rijn ay other. atzthority.—Mr. Wiswall pro- posed, and Mr. Grice seceuded the adoption of the re- commendations.—The were adopted. WATER. COMMITTEE. Recommended by this committee that the seal of the Council be affixed to agreements with the Manchester Ship Canal Company as to wayleaves in respect of water I mains running alongside their docks, &c., and that thel regulations hereby submitted under which the Runcorn Urban District Council will supply .water be approved and adopted and that the seal of the Council be affixed to them ; that application be made on the 6;11 February 1896 to the Local Government .Board for their confirma- tion of such regulations and that the requisite notice be given by advertisement of the intention of this Council to apply for such confirmation.—Mr. Pritchard in mov- ing the adoption of the minutes said it was perhaps necessary to cx.plain that the agreements were the same as existed with the water works company. The Ship Canal Company were desirous of thn agreements being made with the Urban Council. Some of the Council's water pipes passed along the company's docks and the Council paid way leaves. The regulations were made with the object of protecting the interests of the Authority. Toe committee had had before them a num- ber of water regulations in force elsewhere, and they had cone through them with the law clerk who had rendered valuable service in that respect—in adoppne, them to their own requirements. The regulations would be open to the public for inspection for one month. —Mr. Wilson seconded the adoption of the minutes and they were carriei.

THE PAYMENT OF THE RATES. In moving the minutes of the Finance Committee Mr. Lightburn proposed the adoption of the minutes, and stated that the rates were tieing got in very well. It might have come to the knowledge of some members that two or three of them had signed cheques and had been surcharged. He simply mentioned that as leading to something else. They fixed the rate too low last year and they had an overdraft at the bank which they tried to avoid. This overdraft must not become a continuous matter, or they would lose their good repute with the Local Government Beard. Next March when they closed their accounts they must have no arrears. He was torry to make that announcement, but the public might as well know—the sooner the better. By that time they hoped that the cloud of adversity which had so long hung over the town might be dispersed,but the present outlook was not very bright.—Mr. Hewitt seconded.—Mr. Dean said he took exec ption to the remarks of Mr. Lightburn with regard to the Council having fixed the rate too low, and stated that the overdraft had existed for several years.—Mr. Bisbrown said he took it that the overdraft bad been in existenue a number of years, and they had only fol- lowed the practice of previous years. With regard to the surcharge the members need have no fear, for when it was explained to the Local Government Board it would be all right.—Mr. Lighthurn said his point was that if the sate had been bigger, the \ adverse balance would have been paid off.—The minutes were passed. THE COUNT F MEDICAL OFFICER. Mr. Potts Clerk of the County Council wrote inform- ing them that the Council at its meeting on the 14th iilt.had decided to retain the entireservices of Dr.Prancis Vacher, as the County Medical Officer of flealth, and that part of the duties which he had undertaken to per- form was to assist and advise the Urban and Rural Dis- trict Councils in the county and their Medical Officer of Health and Sanitary Inspectors as to the epidemics,&c., and generally to advise them in the performance of their duties when required by them free of charge.—The com- munication was referred to the Health Committee, on the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr.Wiswall. THIS TIDAL OPENThiI AT THE OLD QUAY. The Law Clerk informed the Council that plans and notices had been received from the Ship Canal Company of a Bill to come before Parliament in the ensuing ses- sion. He thought the only effect it had on their district was the filling up of the tidal opening at Old Quay to present the washing of silt and sand into the canal from the river Mersey. Whether it would have an injurious effect on Runcorn he was not prepared to advise.—lt was pointed out by Mr. Pritchard that it had been their cus- tom to submit such matters to the Parliamentary Com- mittee for their consideration. He proposed that it be referred to that committee.—Rev. Dr.Gsstaldi seconded, and it was agreed to.

PRISONERS- FOR NON-PAIMENT OF RATES. The Rev. Dr. Gastaldi stated that a great many people had been summrmed efore the magirtrstes anl sent to prison for the crime sf nut being able to pay rates. He thought it ought to be in the power of the Council to remedy that evil because he was of the opinion that as matters stood it was absurd to deal witO the people at they did. There was no benefit to be derived for sending persons to prison; in fact the result had a contrary effec as the ratepayers had to pay the expenses thus incurred. It was the duty of the authority to look into the ques- tion, and adopt a better practice in vogue else- where. It was a scandalous thing to see people being sent to prison simply because they could not pay their rates—The Clerk : Wha rates ? Rev. Dr. Gastaldi : The poor rates.—Mr. Taylor said the question was entirely out of order. He did not think anyone had been sent to prison for not paying the General District rate.—The Law Clerk pointed out that they had no power at present over the poor rate.—Mr. Taylor said it was out of their province.—The Law Clerk said they had only the control of the District rate, and In connection with that, they could not send a man to prison unless it could he proved that be could pay and ; would not.—Mr. Taylor said there was not a single one summoned for the General District rate.—Rev. Dr. Gas- taldi said in some places the rates were compounded.— Mr. Lightburn said they did that already in Runcorn.— The Chairman said it was clear that Dr. Gastaldi was speaking of an authority over which that Council had no control whatever at present. He did net think it would be profitable to continue the discussion. —The matter then dropped and the Council rose.

FRODSHAIL

FAT STOCK SALE AT FRODSLIAIL —On Thursday the annual show and sale of fat stock promoted by the 1 Frodsham Farmers' Auction Company, Limited, was , held at the yard of the Bear's Paw Hotel. There was a good show and a keen competition resulted for the various prizes offered. The committee in charge of the arrangements were Messrs. J. E. Bate, and Walter Taylor (Sutton), Walter Harrison (Onslow), William Youd, A. Bate, and George Forster (Frodsham). The judges were Messrs. Robert Johnson (Wigan), Frank Harrison (Altrincham), and Dodd (Chester), Their awards were as follows :—Special prizes for the best pair of fat beasts, James Nicholls, Frodsham ; best caw, 1, William Lowe,Alvanley Aall ; 2,William Fowles, Dutton ; commended, Peter Firth, °aglow ; best fat heifer with four broad teeth,:, Griffiths,Clifton Hall, near Runcorn ; 2,5. B.Chadwick, Higher Runcorn ; commmended. T. Lewis, Bradley ; best fat heifer, six broad teeth, 1 and commended, William Griffiths ; best fat bull, 1, William Lowe, Alvanley Hall; com- mended, T. Baxter, C C., Dutton Hall ; best fat bullock, 1 and 2, J. Nicholls, Frodsham ; best fat calf, 1, Willlam Roberts, Woodhouses, near Frodshatn ; commended, Thomas Lewis; best pen of five sheep, Mrs. Robinson, Crabtree Ground, Delamere ; commended, H. M. Davies, Frodsham ; best bull, fed on Messrs. Ashworth's cake, T. Baxter, C.C., Dutton Hall ; best fed beast fed on Mr. Sit - cock's cake, Andrew Hutchinson, Frodsham ; bcst beast fed on Messrs. Ashworth's cake, 1. William Griffiths ; 2, 2, James Nicholls. There was a large attendance at the subsequent sale at which Mr. Hutchinson was the auctioneer. Prices ran-ted as follows ;—Prize animals about £25 ; heifers £lB to £24 ; cows to £23 ; calves £3 to £8 12. ; sheep 355. to £3. The principal buyers were i—Mei-isrs. Tickle, Cotterell (Frodshatn), Shaw, Finnerty, Cross, Coggens, Everett, T. Rigby, Holcroft (Warrington), Savage. Bell, Richardson, Briscow (Runcorn), Hedley, Grocott (Widnes), Bag,uley, B. Mc. Waters (Ditton), Hatton (Lymm). and Dodd (Chester). After the sale dinner was partaken of at the Bear's Paw Hotel, at the invitation of the Auction Company.

TAKING COLD.

A " Cold " is not necessarily the result of a high or low temperature. A person may go from a hot bath directly into a cold one, or into snow even, and not take cold. He may remain out in the coldest atmosphere until chilled through, and still not take cold. On the contrary,he may take cold by pouring a couple of table- spoonfuls of water upon some parts of his dress, or by standing in a door, or before a stove, or by Ent in,g near a window or other opening, where one part of the body is colder than another part. Let it be kept in mind that uniformity of temperature over the whole body is the great thing to be looked after. It is the unequal heat upon different parts of the body that produces Colds, by disturbing the uniform circulation of the blood, which in turn induces congestion of some part. Keep the feet always dry and warm, avoid draughts of air, keep the system braced up by plenty of sleep and good food, and by a powerful Tonic, and you will be proof against a Cold and its results. The very best Medicine, either for preventing or curing a Cold, is

A CRUEL STEPPATHER.—Dtvid Griffiths, 13. Clarence-it!! Barlestown, was charged that on November 6th, and divers other dates, being the guardian of certain children. Thomas, ' James, Charles, Annie. George and Alfred, under the age of 16 years. he ill-treated them so as to cause them unnecessary suffering. Mr. J. W. Knowles, who appeared on behalf of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, said that in January of the year 1894. Griffiths married a widow named Mrs. James, who had four or five children. Boon afte.: they were married, and especially during the past six months, the defendant had continually ill-treated the children by thrashing them and turning them out of doors, with the consequence that the neighbours had to frequently feed the children. A short time previously the wife had summoneii her husband for an assault Out she had subse- quently withdrawn the charge. There were two police officers In the court who would give evidence to the effect that the house was practically destitute of furniture. The children were also badly clothed and were in a very dirty' condition. The wife had not been summoned because she had told a pitiable tile about her husband's conduct, and there- fore the prosecution thought that they would have had a strong witness for the case. That morning, however, her views appeared to have been changed and she now said that her husband was the kindest husband possible. However, if she was called by her husband, he would have an opportunity of cross-examining her, and they would be in a condition to show that she was committing perjury.—lnspector McAteer, N.S.P.O C., was then called and stated that in consequence of complaints made to hini on the 4th of November, he went to defendant's house in company with P.C. Green. He there found the wife and children, who appeared to be almost destitute. He examined the children who were insufficiently clad and were in a very poor condi- tton. On the bed upstairs where the children slept, there was only one sheet, and all four had to sleep In this bed. The woman accounted for the state of the house and children by saying that she was obliged to pawn the furniture to get food for her children and herself, Her husband was frequenVy drunk, and turned out the children. and also smashed up the furniture.—P.C. Burns, and P.C. Green gave corrobora-

Got- borne. On the day in question the three defendants with others were Inn public-house playing dominoes. She was then kicked and otherwise assaulted and put on the fire. In de- fence Mr. Knowles ssid that if Mrs. Thompson was struck at all it was in the scuffle, but as to being put on the fire that charge was absolutely denied. Witnesses were called, and the Bench ultimately convicted George Unsworth and fined him 10s. and costs. They also convicted the two other de- fendant s. and lined them 20s. and casts each. _

• "HAVE YOU BEEN VACCINATED ? "

"What a question to ask a doctor who has this morn- ing vaccinated twenty-three cases !" " In this matter," my friend answered, " yeu doctors and the public are in the same position. Come now you know perfectly well that it is the doctors' children who are most often not vaccinated, because the doctors know beat the dangers of the diseases that follow vaccination, and the utter inability of the profession to cure those diseases. Is it not true," he continued, "that since the days of Ihctor Telmer many of you have sought, but never discovered, a cure for those dreadful diseases which blight and destroy so many children yearly,and leave many more to grow up physi- cal wrecks,covered with blotches, pimples, papules,sores about the ears and mouth and in the hair, tortured with eczema, inflam6d eyelids, and many diseases of bones and internal organs, all of which are both obstinate and severe, and all tt e result of vaccination ?"

"A heavy accusation you bring against us doctors," I replied. "True," he answered, "but one borne out by facts. You have all done the best you could, but you must ad- mit that you have all failed." "I am compelled to admit it, because you and all the world know it to be true. The reason of our failure is this : Nature intended that all diseasee should be contracted either by swallowing or inhaling germs, most of which would thus be either weakened or des- troyed before they entered the blood, aud she provided herbs for the cure of all these diseases. In vaccination the germs are carried by the vaccinator's knife directly into the blood without any weakening",r thinning. The diseases resulting thereby were not provided for by Na- ture, and so we have no remedy for them. It is Nature's fault, not the doctors',"

" Hold !"be cried, "there you are wrong. Nature, assisted by man, a great man and a great doctor, has discovered an absolute preventive and cure, not a single herb, but a combination of herbs scientifically com- pounded and carefully tested. In hospital and private practice it has been succcssfully used, both as a preven- tive and as a cure, without a single failure. Give it." he said, as he handed me a bottle of Vaccination Virus Vanquisher, or V.V.V., " to those you are vaccinating, and your patients will no longer dread the operation, as it will enter the blood unchanged from the stomach, and by its antiseptic action destroy all the germs before they have had any opportunity to injure the constitution. Give it to those who are suffering from the effects of your previous vaccinations and they will be speedily cured. The accompanying testimonials will satisfy you, but a careful trial will positively convince you." I found the testimonials so satisfactory, and his argu- ments so reasonable, that, although I am an old prac- titioner, I commenced using it at once, and now use it largely every day of my life. Up to the present it has not failed once, either as a preventive or as a cure, and I am convinced that "V.V.V." is a great discovery, and a boon to both the public and the conscientious doctor. —" The Modern Family Doctor." The price 2s. 9d. per bottle, places it within the reach of all. It may be obtained from all Chemists, or from the V.V.V. Company, Limited, 31, Snow Hill, London, E.C., who will send a pamphlet on the subject to all who apply.

ROMAN CATHOLICS AND THE EDITCA-

TION Q GESTION.

, Cardinal Vaughan and his brother prelates have issued a declaration on the Roman Catholic position in regard to the education controversy. They say that they are unab:e to undertake, like the Anglican Church, a great annual contribution towards the maintenance of public elementary schools. For the larger number of Catholic schools it is impossible to ensure continued voluntary contributions in any fixed proportion,and they demand that the whole cost of maintenance in their public el.‘- mentary schools shall be defrayed by the State—a de- mand which, they declare, is constitutional in principle and in actual accordance with Parliamentary legislation in the case of Ireland,and also in the case of English and Scotch reformatory schools. L'ird Russell of KiHowell (Lord Chief Justice), speaking at a meeting of Roman Catholics in London on Tuesday evening, said the State must recognise the inalienable right of children to he educated in the religion of their parents. Mr. Justin M'Carthy, M.P. who was also among the speakers, em- phatically declared, in the name of the Catholics in Par - liament, that in this matter there was for them abso- lutely no surrender.

WARRINGTON v. TYLDESLEY.

For some weeks past the Warrington first team have been singularly unfortunate, suffering defeat Limn after time, but only as a rule,by a point or two. Tyldesley, whom they played on Saturday, were in a somewhat similar position,having lost several matches lately. The meeting of the two teams was bound to change the luck of one of them, and Warrington turned out to be the favoured team. There were two changes on the selected team, flouehton and O'Brien taking the places of Bate and Nevins at half-back, this being O'Brien's second appearance in that position. Tyldesley had the team they have played for the past two or three weeks. The game was a peculiar one from start to finish, but as it went in favour of Warrington there is no reason to complain of that. The visitors played very strongly all through, while Tyldesley played loosely and gave their opponents opportunities which they were not slow to take advantage of. The weather was of a most disagree- able character, being cold arid damp, and a mist hung over the field which almost prevented half the game being seen by the spectators. The ground in parts was also very sloppy owing to the recent rain and this was certainly not couducive to good or accurate play. The teams were as fellows : Warrington : Heesom,full back ; Barber (captain),Cross, Burton and Broady, three•quarters ; Houghton and O'Brien. half backs ; Burrows, Barrett, Andrews,Taylor, Swift, Fairhurst, Wilcock and Hill, forwards. Tyldesley: A. Trow, full back ; Lawton, Worthington, Miller and Elliott. three-quarters; J. Berry and W. Berry, half backs ; Woodward (captain), 'fin ler. Shepherd, Fox, J. Roberts. W. Roberts, Berry and Mullooney, forwards, The Warrington team were accompanied by about a Lundred of their supporters, the railway company run- ning a special train. The unfavourable weather doubt- less prevented many football enthusiasts accompanyieg the team. The kick off was announced for three o'clock but it was a quarter past before Woodcock, for Tyldes- lev, set the hall in motion. Warringten made a splendid return, but kicks by Miller, Lawton and Worthington took the ball back into Warrington quarters, and the homesters securing two free kicks within a few minutes of each other began to look dangerous. From the last free kick, the home forwards passed, and Warrington relieved the pressure by touching down. Ther,however, soon made amends for this by invading the home quar- ters, and making a determined attack on the home goal, Burton, Barber and Broady putting in some useful work. The first named who had taken O'Brien's place at half back, played with exceptional vigour and was in no small degree instrumental in obtaining the victory in which the game ended. Some loose play at centre ended in Warrington gaining an advantage, and the Tyldesley , backs not fielding the ball well O'Brien got over the line near the corner, and to the astonishment of the Tyldesleptes Burton converted the point into a goal. Elated by their success, Warrington played up splendidly, the home team being overplayed at all points, and it was not long ere Barber getting possession managed to circumvent the Tyldesley backs and place the ball under the goal posts. Burton again took the place kick and landed goal number two. Half-time was shortly afterwards called, with Warrington leading by twe goals to nothing. The news was, as usual, wired to the Wilderspool ground and the reply quickly came back, "Don't believe it." In the second half, Warring- ton played more on the defensive. With two goals to their credit they could afford to do so. The play gener- ally was of a low order. As the result of a scramble near the Warrington line, " Buff " Berry got over but he was held by two IVarrington players before he could ground the ball, and the referee ordered a scrimmage. Berry again succeeded in getting over, and this time grounded near the corner flag. Lawton tried for goal but failed. The rest ef the game was of an uninteresting character, and time was called with the score, Warring- ton two goals, Tyldesley one try. The Tyldesleyites claimed that the referee had stopped the game several minutes before time, but there must have been some misapprehension. Half-an-hour each way was played, and in the second half, half-an-hour was certainly played. The referee, Mr. Ingle. of Stockport, at times I during the game seemed a little nervous, and once or twice revised his decisions, but he certainly let the teams I play the time agreed upon. After leaving the field it is said that one or two roughs tried to assault the referee, and Woodward. the Tyldesley captain, knocked one of I them into a ditch.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

meeting of the General Committee of the Northern Union was held at the Spread Eagle Hotel, Manchester on Tuesday evening. Mr. J. E. Warren (Wairington) presided. A resolution was passed that S. Hall late of Swinton, he allowed to play under the rules of the Northern Union.Sotne time ago Hall went abroad,and be- fore going was presented with a money testimonial by the Swinton Club. This made him a professional under the rules of the English Rugby Union. Returning, he applied to be reinstated, but his application was refused. It is not known which cluNin the Northern Union, Hall will play with. Referees were instructed to report cases of late starts, stating which club is in fault ; and it was de- cided that such clubs should bg'fined at the discretion of the Committee. This resolution will take effect from the 14th instant. The remainder of the business was of a routine character.

ST. PETER'S C.L.B. V. SANKEY C.L.B.—This match was played on Saturday on the Rectory ground. San- key won the toss,and Holbrook kicked off for the Saints against a strong wind. During the first half the Saints pressed hard,and Fairhurst was called upon many times to save. Half time soon arrived with no score. In the second half play was very fast.each goal being visited in turn. In the last five minutes Holbrook gave Gregory a grand pass,that player sending in a fine shot,which beat the Sankey goalkeeper. Soon after the whistle sounded for time, and the Saints were left victorious by one goal to nil.

HOLY TRINITY, WARRINGTON V. FRODSRAM.—This match was played at Frodsham on Saturday. In point of physique there was no comparison in the two teams, the Frodsham team being a powerful combination com- pared to the wiry Warringtonians. ID:waver, what the latter lacked in strength was more than counterbalanced in scientific methods,for throughont the game Frodsham failed to cope with the superior tactics of the visitors. At the onset the home side scored a goal,and maintained the lead for some considerable time, but eventually the Warringtonians drew level as a result of excellent com- bination, whilst in the latter stages of the game the visitors claimed almost a monopoly of the attack, and their efforts culminated in another goal, which gave the Trinitarians the victory by two goals to one.

EVERTON COMBINATION V. LIVERPOOL AND DISTRICT COMBINATION.—The above teams met to give an exhibi- tion of Association football on the St. Elphin's ground.' Wilderspool Causeway,on Saturday. MR. CHAMBERLAIN AND BRITISH TRADE.

As the result of consultations with the High Commis- sioner for Canada, the Agents General of the other self- governing colonies, and the principal Chambers of Commerce in the United Kingdom, Mr. Chamberlain has drawn up a despatch on the subject of the trade between the United Kingdom and the colonies, and has forwarded a copy to all the Colonial Governors. He says he wishes to investigate thoroughly the extent to which in each of the colonies foreign imports of any kind have displaced or are displacing similar British goods and the causes of such displacement, and he asks for information which will assist in securing as large a share as possible of the mutual trade of the United Kingdom and the colonies for British manufacturers and producers, hether located in the colonies or in the United Kingdom.

ANOTHER SHOCKING OUTRAGE ON A

BOY.

Ancther shocking crime was perpetrated in London on Tuesday. A little boy named Cattle was missed by his mother, who, in view of the fact that a child was mur- dered in the same locality two months ago, became greatly alarmed for his safwty. A diligent search was made, and in a stable, the door of which had to be forced open, the poor child was at length found securely tied up in a sack beneath a pile of market baskets. There were wounds in the chest and abdomen, and had the discovery been delayed a little longer the child must have perished. The inquiries of the police resulted in the discovery that a youth named Gamble, who was arrested for the previous crime but discharged for want of evidence, was the last person seen in the child's com- pany, and the police at once rearrested him. The boy Gamble, who is charged with the attempted murder of a little boy named Cattel in Islington, London on Tuesday night, and who is also strongly suspected of murdering another little boy in the same neighbour- hood two months ago, was brought up at tha Clerken- well Police Court on Wednesday, and remanded. The prisoner's only answer to the usual question from the Bench was that there was a glass roof to the stable in which the child was found, and that anybody could get in by moving the glass.

Lord Salisbury has followed up the gift of Heligoland to the Germans by handing over to them the small island of Mondoleh, on the West African coast. Arthur Covington was executed at Bedford on Tues- day for the murder a his cousin, Effie Burgin, to whom he had been paying attention.
