NEW BOOKS, MAGAZINES, ETC.

CASSELL AND Co.—The usual sixpence purchases the Christmas number of Cassell's magazine, but with it you get reading matter to the value of half- as-much again. Not only is the magazine proper more than usually attractive but there is thrown in "free,gratia,and for nothing," an illustrated supple- ment of 16 pages which are filled with a story of Australian adventure entitled"lrralie'sßushranger." Mr. E. W. Horming is the author of the story—one of excitement and interest and possessing all the attraction Mr. Hornuing is supposed to be able to endow his contributions with. Among the items in the first part of the magazine, which this month enters upon its 22nd year of publication, are a warmly coloured print of a drawing by S E. Wal- ler, entitled " Love's Messenger," 'The Devil's Manuscript," complete story by S. Levett Yeats; the opening chapters of a continued story by that powerfully dramatic veriter,Mr. Frank Barrett, en- titled " A Missing Witness " ; " Freaks and Tricks in Handwriting," by an expert; and many ether similar gems of literature. An excellent prize com- petition has been devised, providing prizes from .£1 to £2O to the person who indicates most success- fully the relative popularity of the contents of the December part and the person who gives the best answer to the quest'on as to which item he likes best; a further four prizes are offered for the best suggestions of three subjects for the coming voiume.—Other magazines by the same publishers which it they have not all achieved popularity, are at least valued for their merits, are " Cassell'a Natural History," by Dr. P. M. Duncan, F.R.S. (part 62), which promises to be one of the most complete and capably written books of its kind yet extant; " Picturesque America" (part 33) is an education in itself, and provides a chronicle of America's natural beauties, many of which are reproduced, which has never before been attempted with such success in this country ; "Our Earth and its Story " (part 35), merits the same description,and is edited by Dr. Robert Brown, MA., F.R.0.5., F.L.S. ; " Cas- sell's Gazetteer "of Great Britain and Ireland (part 27), which, when complete, will be invaluable as a complete topographical dictionary of the United Kingdom, containing numerous illustrations and 60 maps; " Cassell's New Popular Educator" (part 15), gives information and teaching in almost all branches of knowledge, as well as combines pro- fuse illustrations with some 50 plates and maps printed in colours;" The Queen's London "(part 4) one of the newest of the periodicals from this office, provides all England with 384 faithful views of the metropolis and its environs cleanly printed. A cheap edition of "Science for All " by Robert Brown, M.A., Ph. D., F.L.S., F.R.G.S., has been com- menced this month. Each part is to contain 96 pages and the whole volume of 20 parts will con- tain over 1,700 illustrations. This edition at 6d. per month puts within the reach of all, scientific knowledge which as well as being interesting is I bound to be of use sometime or other. Good paper is used in the production of "Science for All," and the printing is all that could be desired at the price. C. Ammon PEAnsore.—This enterprising pub- lisher of popular periodicals, brings before the notice of the public another sixpenny magazine, bearing his familiar name as a "hall-mark,' viz, " Pearson'e Magazine." We have always noticed in reviewing magazines of this stamp that they so fairly bristle with an impenetrable armour of good points that we cannot anyhow "pick holes in them "—not that we have any wish to do so other than by faik criticism—and the newest arrival does not seem to have departed from the rule of its many illustrious predecessors. To begin with ,number one contains over 100 pages of articles, stories, and sketches by such prominent writers as Anthony Hope, Bret Harte, Sir Walter Besant, W. L. Alden, Robert Barr, Barry Pain, Archibald Forbes, Allan Upward, etc., etc.; further, among the illustrators are R. Caton Woodville, A. Forestier, J. F. Sullivan, Hal Hurst,R.Sauber,Chris Hammond, G. G.Manton,Charles May,etc., etc. A series of articles which commences in No. lis " Artists and their Work." being brief notes on prominent painters, with the portraits of some, and representative ex- amples of their work; another is " What it costs " —the public likes to be "in the know" of what a familiar thing costs—and this series explains the working expense of various companies, etc., begin- ning with the London and North-Western Railway Company, and some remarkable facts are given. "Secrets of the Courts of Europe," also appeals to the taste of everyone who takes the slightest inter- est in home and foreign administration as well as In the private affairs of royalty. The remaining budget of contents is too extensive and varied to attempt to detail and we can only go on to the end of the number where the editor is allowed a little latitude to make his announcements. Here he says if" Pearson's Magazine" is not the best sixpenny- worth that has hitherto been produced it will be a failure—frem our view of its quality we cannot entertain the idea of its failure—and he adds that the highest point has not yet been reached but ex- presses confidence in it being attained,and promises that succeeding numbers will surpass this first essay both in interest and appearance. Several of the forthcoming dainties are mentioned and we can only conclude by expressing our desire to taste them, and also our humble opinion that consider- able ingenuity will have to be exercised before any improvement is effected. MACMILLAN AND CO.—Two more of the People's edition of Tennyson', works have been issued in " Locksley Hall," and" A Dream of Fair Women," each containing several smaller poems. For the benefit of those who may not yet have seen this series booklete,we are pleased to state that they are essentially neat and handy, and their. individual price is ls.—Of the Kingsley Pocket edition, "Two Years Ago "is the latest arrival. Like the preced- ing numbers it is of some 300 pages, lightly but clearly printed, and quietly bound in blue cloth. Price Is. 6d.—Another of the Illustrated Standard Novels is Jane Austen's "Pride and Prejudice," with first-class sketchy illustrations by Charles E. Brock, who gave such satisfaction by his treatment of the scenes in " The 'annals of the Parish and the Aryshire Legatees." Mr. Austin Dobson furnishes in his introduction to the book, an interesting out- line of the public and private career of Miss Austen both before and after her production of " Pride and Prejudice," rightly regarded as her masterpiece. Mr. Dobson says "To say nothing of the supreme excellence of the dialogue, there is scarcely a page but has its little gem of exact and polished phras- ing; scarcely a chapter which is plot adroitly opened or artistically ended Criticism has found little to condemn in the details of this capital novel."—The "Century and "St Nicholas" magazines for December are special Christmas numbers and in addition to the standard class of literature which they usually contain, are articles, stories, or sketches, suitable to the period of the year and the character of the number. The most important of these in the " Century "is an article on Tissot's " Life of Christ "by Edith Coues, which is not only an example of capable treatment of the subject in hand, but the illustrations pertaining to it are beautiful in the extreme, being reproduced by permission of Messrs. Mame and Son,of Tours. The frontispiece to the magazine is "Jesus found in the Temple" from the painting by James Tissot.—" The History of Mankind"has now reached part 3,and fur ther evidence is afforded by it that this history by F.Ratzel is going to beef extreme value as a reliable chronicle of all pertaining to the human race from its commencement, up to date. Various races are dealt with, and the whole subject is treated in a very capable manner. A richly coloured plate of Malay fabrics and weapons prefaces the number, which also contains numerous illustrations of mem- bers of uncivilized tribes, their implements and commoner utensils, &c. Thirty parts of is, each will complete the volume. TER " QUEEN " PUBLISHING COMPANY.—The Christmas number of the" Queen,"the ladies' news- paper, does not strike us as being so much superior to the ordinary edition as it might be. A few leas special articles on subjects certainly prominent,but somewhat stale, with a like moderation of chronic- lism of events,would we venture to think,have been more of an attraction for Christmas reading, par- ticularly if they were replaced by items of a more literary character dashed with Yule-tide cheerful- ness. What articles and short stories there are, are good and creditable to the tone of the number, but it is the tone of the number that could have been advantageously varied. Amusements, music, art, fashions, Sec., are accorded their quota of space and will be of benefit to many. No fault can be found with the plates presented in the edition,"His Lord- ship," from the picture of W. R. Symonds, and "The Gipsy Queen?! The former is a faithful representa- tion of the original and the latter is a toned photo- graphic reproduction. Both make pleasing wall decorations.

MR. R. H. PEARSON'S CONCERT.

On Wednesday evening week Mr. R. 11. Pearson gave a concert in the:Parr Hall,Warrington,at which it was originally intended that the late Sir Charles Halle and Lady Halle should have made their first appearance before a Warrington audience. The lamented death of the former, however, intervened and necessitated changes in the arrangements, which, though probably the best that could be made under the circumstances, must be held accountable for somewhat damping public interest, The modified prospect and the fact that the substi- tuted principals were comparatively unknown will therefore explain why the attendance at the concert was far from satisfactory. It was, however, doubly unfortunate that the concert itself, regarded music- ally, failed to realise reasonable expectations. Miss Sarah Berry and Herr Brodsky (the new leader of Sir Charles Halle's orchestra) must be chiefly credited with having saved the concert from mediocrity,although it cannot be said that as a- solo violinist the latter made the most of his oppor- tunity for establishing a reputation before a new and critical audience. The " Ciaconna " of J. S Bach may be admitted to be a severe test of tech- nique, but it has little interest for the many to whom perfection of method in violin playing is a matter of indifference. There is smafi scope in such a solo for the display of those emotional qualities appertaining to the artiste's personality without which he is powerless to move an audience or to call forth any responsive sympathy. Only in the "Cradle song"( Simon) and Beethoven's sonata had the audi- ence any means of guaging Herr Brodsky's capabili- ties, but it must be recorded that in these he re- ceived such unstinted proof of the audience's appre- ciation as to leave no doubt of the high opinion held in regard to his playing. Miss Berry sang with finished taste the songs set down in the programme but, cs not infrequently happens, the items not an- nounced rendered in response to encores,were those which gave most pleasure to the listeners. The two vocal morceaux by Dvorak, scholarly though they may be, are distinctly disappointing in the concert roum.:Tbe "Shepherd's Cradle Song" (Somervell), while having much in common with these songs has also much more in it, both in the ' sentiment and the setting, to rivet attention and excite interest; and in this and "My heart is weary," from Goring Thomas's Nadeshda,Miss Berry evinced such versatility of style and complete command of a rich voice.that the audience would take no denial of further favours. Sullivan's " Willow Song" and "Robin Adair" were substituted and gave rise to renewed expressions of approval. Miss Ethel Wood is too immature in voice and style to deal adequ- ately with the difficult songs she essayed, and there was in her singing evidence of an over-straining which not only tends to faulty intonation but threatens to establish marked and ruinous distinc- tions in the registers of her voice. Miss Wood has however the merit of clear enunciation coupled with a vivacious manner, and she was thus able to impart a freshGess to the encore song "A Little Mountain Lad " (Roeckel) which won for her ungrudging applause. The violoncello solos by Mr. Walter Barton, while not of the highest order, gave evidence of the performer's undoubted ability as an executant. The shortcomings were more in the way of failure to catch the deeper and subtler meanings of the composer than any inability to cope with the technical diffi- culties pertaining to the instrument, and we are not sure that Mr. Barton did not suffer from slight nervousness. As might be expected, Chopin's noc- turne was least satisfactory,t he more florid passages lin this pathetic solo receiving too flippant a render- big to be entirely in keeping with its character But Mr. Hatton is young, and has a world of possibilities before him, and he may, therefore,be reasonably expected to surmount these obstacles with the ripening of his powers and his judgment. The polonaise in D minor (Popper) and Dunkler's " Caprice Hongroise " both took immensely with the audience. Of the performance of the solo pianist, Madame Frickenhaus, we can speak finally in terms of the highest praise. The Waldstein sonata was a great treat, although we must dis- claim any sanction of the slackened tempo on the entry and recurrence of the chorale subject in the first movement. The Finale was very effective, and the interpretation of the whale work marked by musicianly taste and a high standard of technique. Mr. Eric A. Chapman played the accompaniments to the various songs and solos.

MR. W. H. JUDE AT THE PARR HALL.

I Notwithstanding the very inclement weather of Thurs Iday week there was a very satisfactory attendance at ; the Parr Hall, as a result of the announcement that Mr. W. H. Jude, the well-known organist of Liverpool, and the Gwalia Male Voice Choir were providing the musical repertoire. Mr. Jude's fame is too well-known to be dilated upon, while the Welsh choir of 25 voices above mentioned has also earned a high roputation as a band of skilled vocalists. The proceeds were in aid of the Welsh Church, Crosfleld-st. The Rev. Mr. Jones, minister of the church gave the announcement, which was received with regret by the audience, that a telegram had been received from Mr. P. B. Scott's doctor at Falleweeld, ' stating that Mr. Scott had caught a chill which had ter- minated in a slight attack of pleurisy, and he was thus unable to fulfil his engagement to act as chairman. Owing to Mr. Scott's indisposition the lady singers he was bringing with him from Manchester were also un- able to appear. The following programme was then gone through :—" Militaire," (Gounod). Mr. Jude; H Valiant Warrior," (Jenkins), choir; "Le cloche du Soir, (Chuvet), Mr. Jude; "Spartan Heroes," (Brothe- roe), choir; " Joccata," in G major, (Dubois), Mr. ' Jude; "There's a flower that bloometb,"Mr.A.Atkins of Warrington ; "Venetian Gondola song," (Bennett), Mr. , Jude; " Pilgrim's chorus," choir ; " Legends et Final symphonique " (Guilmant), Mr. Jude ; " Good-bye Sweetheart," Mr. Atkins ; " Sailor's chorus," choir ; "Trumpet March," ccmposed and played by Mr. Jude. The organist treated his hearers to a variety of music which can only be described as of the highest class, cembining as it did,the subdued and spirited in harmony ;by a master hand. Several encores were demanded of Mr. Jude, and which when accorded convinced the audience that they were a good investment. His final piece "The Trumpet March," one of his own composition, and though short,was fulllof beauty not only in its mar- tial spiritedness, but also in the musical harmony of the whole. The "Venetian Gondola song" was also accepted ass pleasing break in the more classical pieces which preceded it. The Gwalia choir also acquitted itself with all credit, its especial features being perfect harmony of voice and nice precision. Their conductor, was Mr. J. A. Roberts, and the accompanist, was Mr. W. J. Roberts. The only member of the choir who rendered a solo was Mr. Barry Roberts whose version of the "Village Blacksmith," was well received and encored. As a musical programme, that on Thursday night was superior to many previously given in Warrington and well worthy the patroeage it received.

THE WARRINGTON FLOTI4L AND HORTICULTURAL SOCIETY. MR. J. CRaSFIELD'S GIFT OF BULBS.

, A meeting of the committee of this society was held at the offices, Scotland-road, on Thursday week, the chair being occupied by Councillor Smethurst. The treasurer, Mr. J. Fulton Smith, presented the balance sheet for the past year, which had been audited by I Councillor J. Shaw Green, and from it, it appeared that the year commenced with a balance in hand of £ll 115 s. 10d. Subscriptions for the year amounted to £7O 1199. 6 d. • entrance fees, £7 4s. ; advertisement fees, £2 193. 6J. ; takings at tb., shows, £23 17s. 3d. ; bank interest, is. ; programmes sold, £lO 39. ; tickets sold, I£7 10s. 6d.; total, £124 17s. 10d. The expenditure had been £lll 183. Bd., which left a balance in hand on the year's working of £l2 193.2 d. Mr. Ryan, the secretary, said that be had considered that the balance Was very good, especially when he told them that the receipts had been £2O less than the previous year.— Councillor Smethurst, in moving the adoption of the balance sheet,said that he was sure they all were pleased to bear the result of the show. He was sure that they I owed a hearty vote of thanks to Mr. Ryan and Mr. J. Fulton Smith for their:labours as secretary and treasurer, land he did not think they could get two better gentle- men anywhere. He would have liked to have heard a ;vote of thanks passed to them at the show,but he hoped they would accept the will for the deed. lie trusted that in the future the society would receive the support it deserved,and that other gentlemen would emulate the example of Mr. John Crosfleld who bad so generously offered to give the bulbs and also prizes for the help of I the society. He coupled with the motion for the adop- tion of the balance sheet, a hearty vote of thanks to IMessra. Ryan and Smith.—Mr. Calder seconded the motion which was carried unanimously. It was decided Ito hold a general meeting of subscribers shortly. From lain advertisement it will be seen that in accordance with the arrangements made by Mr. John Crosfield, J.P., a free distribution of hyacinths bulbs to the workingmen and working women within the borough ef Warrington will be made by the Warrington Horticultural Society. These bulbs will be distributed on the distinct under. standing that they will be grown and produced on the day of the show. Prizes for the best production el flower spikes will be given, full particulars of which will be Issued along with the bulbs.

At the Derby Borough Police Court on Monday William Clark, landlord of the High-st. Tavern, was fined £5O, with the alternative of three months' impri- sonment, and his wife was fined £2O, with the elterna- ' tive of two months' imprisonment,for keeping their pre- mises open for the purpose of betting.

Kettero to the Mitor.

TIIE NEWEST FARTHING.-A WARNING.

Sir,—! should esteem it a great favour if you would give a word of warning to the public respecting a fraud which is being somewhat extensively practised. The new farthing (1895 issue) has a striking resemblanco to a half sovereign,although of course, differing widely from that coin on close inspection. It is,however, well calcu- lated to deceive a person in the hurry of businef-, espe- cially by artificial light, so that shopkeepers and others would do well to be upon their guard.—Thanking you in anticipation, I remain, sir, faithfully yours, LUKE TALBOT, Chief Constable.

Sir,—Will you kindly permit me to respectfully in. form J. H. of Whitecross Ward that I suggested the use of the Old Barracks as a horse fair only, believing that the people of the town would be in favour of a market for financial reasons, and because it it were adopted it would give the people a good chance ti study the work- ing of a market in general, in what seemed to be the most reasonable, the most convenieat, and the least ex- pensive place ;or to prove to all that a cattle market is really not properly workable in the town. RAFFLING AT BAZAARS.

Sir,—There has lately been a good deal said in the local newspapers, and considerably more privately, about raffling, and the sale of iatoxicants at the church bazaar recently held in the Tarr Hall. My reason for writing this letter is, not that I think it will make any difference to the non-abstainer, to whom one glass more or less can't make ranch difference, but to prevent, if possible, a re- currence of some of. the disgraceful and saddening scenes ever being repeated, in connection with religious (?) movements. One of the "scenes" to which I particu- larly with,to refer, was that of a number of boys, or youths, conducting themselves in a very foolish manner, to say the least of it,in consequence of some of the num- ber having won, and partaken freely of, two bottles of wine, port and sherry, in a raffle, the fee for memeership being a few coppers.lt is bad enough Loyale the wines, but when boys drink them until they are scarcely res- ponsible for their conduct (purely out of a spirit of bra- vado, it may be) it is quite time that the temptation and danger were out of their reach. It is much to he regret- ted that " our boys" can't go to a church baziar without exposing themselves to such a baneful influence.—Yours truly, PATERFAMILIAS.

===== 0002601_18951207_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REV. E. T. REED AND SUNDAY CONCERTS.

Sir,—The Rev.E.T. Reed has brought himself into pro- minence during the present week by his sermo, at the I Emmanuel Church on Sunday evening, in which he ' vigorously denounced the Warrington Corporation for its action in allowing Parr Hall to be used for the pur- poses of a sacred concert on Sunday, Nov. 17. No one who knows him can doubt Mr. Reed's sincerity and honesty of purpose, but I consider that on this occasion Ihe is showing a little too much bigotry in the matter. His zeal is mis-directed and there are many real evils lagainst which he might with advantage turn his Italents and attention. For what are the circumstances of the case? The concert in question did not clash with any other public service, as it was held after the churches and chapels had finished. I would like to know what Mr. Reed would prefer the young people to do— to spend the rest of the evening walking Sankey-st., or Wilderspool Causeway, and thus being a nuisance to everybody, or perhaps in a public. house, rather than at the Parr Hall listening to music—sacred music—which as no doubt Mr. Reed himself admits, tends to elevate the mind? Young people are much better employed in listening to a splendid concert, than in running riot in the public streets. Perhaps Mr. Reed has not considered this fact. understand that the Warrington Corporation ha; been threatened by some society or other with prosecution in consequence of holding the concert in question. But I should like to know why prosecutions have not been started in Blackpool, Scarborough, Brighton, and all the other popular watering places in the country. In these places, concerts are regularly held on Sundays and mind you,in most cases for private gain. Warrington which has scarcely one Sunday concert in the year ought surely to be left in peace while these ether places are not attacked. ' Another point I would like to mention is that Mr. Reed attempts to make a strong point of the fact that the law is broken by the holding of this concert,and that thereby the Corporation weakens Its power ot enforcing other laws, and mentions among others the Weights and Measures Act. Why ! the law in question was passed in 1781, or 114 years ago, and people have nowadays be- come a little more enlightened than they were then. There are many old Acts still unrepealed which are broken every day. Mr. Reed himself breaks one of them every Sunday—not one solitary Sunday but every Sun- day—by not attending the established church. There is still an unrepealed Act,which of course is never enforced, which orders under fear of pains and penalties, every one to attend the parish church at least once every Sun- day. This shows that Mr. Reed's contention that the Corporation are wrong simply because they contravene an ancient law which is not now enforced, is not a .very weighty one.—Yours truly, Warrington, November 28th, 1895.

J. 13.

WARRINGTON CATTLE MARKET.

Sir,—The second contribution from J.H., of " eawr ward, d'ye moind," renders the meaning of his first more clear and justifies the more, my last, to which Ism prepared to add the " convincing matter " requested. J.H., will observe from your first page advertisements that the Town Clerk has given formal notice that the half yearly cattle and horse fairs will—for the time be- ing, of course—be held on the Corporation's land lying between Winwick-road and Orford-lane. Just so,but in the temporary sense only, for the people of that congested area will still hope for and eventually realise , the growth of a park on that ground as a branch or I nursery of our grand central one. What such a creation in the midst of squalid environment would prove to thousands, and particularly to women, children and in- valids, cannot be over-estimated. Let everyone inter- ested attend the fairs, and should it prove fairly representative, it will be at once seen that J.ll. 's sug- gestion is useless in its size defect alone, without offering means of expansion with the growth of time and^ things. That the residents in the vicinity of the Barracks object to the use of the same as a cattle or horse market everybody has long known, but I deny in lull that the cardinal point before the electors of any ward recently contested was the one we here discuss. It was distinctly before the electors of St. Austin's Ward but either can- didate would have opposed the proposal it returned, as the present member does. It did not decide the election. Wherever the site :nay be chosen numbers will more or less opposed. The line of least resistance is not always the wisest,because the easiest to follow. The fair would really prove no such nuisance as J.H. so positively affirms. For many years,almost from their inception,ttie fairs have been held in the leading thoroughfare like Winwick-st., and a change of venue is now ch!efly sought because of the claims of ordivary traffic and the better arrangements of a walled area,than an important highway. As between the Barracks and our friend's suggestion, I hold to the contents of my last, despite his additional fervour. The place he suggests would not prove lees offensive, while the Barracks are public property and seem to be designed for the purpose. The land he pro- poses could, no doubt, be easily obtained, but it lies level with the gullet at its north end and level with the lane at its west, offering difficulties everywhere. The testimony of a leading Corporation official is that the site in question is "ludicrously impossible,"*for more Trims reasons even than its small size. Th, 'arracks with the large field near them are nearly thric large as T. H.'s beautiful selection. As for the floc .og of the sewers at certain tides that would prove such a place a natural flushing process. Museum-street was referred to as a useful ground. The day school was taken account of, scholars could enter and depart from the side door

particular market days. The road could be fenced and--,, thus a playground be found for the children, while thh` reit of the road, even if lined at, any time, which deftibtful, by horses would suffer nothing from a few? horse canters. As to bis special plea (quite compre. hended) on behalf of the residential quarter (apart from I all other considerations, mind ye), let J. H. know that to-day the whole town with its villadom, cottages and slummeries is to be regarded as a residential quarter, and that any quotable area is as much to be protected from the " nuisance" he so hotly sedge to bring to his own already afflicted ward as the rest—Yours truly, MACKINTOSFI DAYFRANK.

Warrington.

RR MESSRS. BISHOP AND CO., AND THE CORPORATION. Sir,—My name having been mentioned in Messrs. Bishop and Co.'s letter, printed in last Saturday's "Examiner," I feel that I ought in justice to myself to

state my reasons publicly wby I object to the land in Wharf-street being sold. . . .

Ist. This street has only been partially formed, one footpath only having been made ; and as the street is narrow, the Corpor.ttion has recently purchased pro- perty to widen it, and it would be an anomaly., if they buy land to widen, and sell land to narrow the street, at one and the same time.

2nd. The total width of footpath, street paved, also land in qusstion, is not more than 42 feet altogEther, measuring from river frontage. of Roberts Dale crane. Now in the Public Health Act, 1875, form G, schedule 4, the byelaws state " That all streets are to be made 40 feet wide, and that the carriage way and footpaths mast be paved." I therefore object most strongly to public rights of streets being sold for the behefit of private companies. 3rd. There is plenty of land to be sold, abuttine, upon the river in the immediate neighbourhood, which will meet all Messrs. Bishop and Co.'s requirements, without interfering with the rights of the public.

'4th. The CorporatiOn proposes tojerect a crane, cl4se adjoining, to discharge their own stone as well as goods for the public. .

sth. If boats are to be discharged alongside the pro- posed wharf ,there will be great danger to boats navigat- ing down the river, of dropping across the pillars of the bridge, especially when a flood, or fresh is in the river, owing to che very sharp curve at that point, combined with insufficiency of width of river. . .

I object to any erection interfering with the hauling path. In reply to Messrs. Bishop and Co'a. last paragraph, representations were made to the Manchester Ship Canal .Company about two years ago, stating the urgent necessity fora wharf at the entrance to the Warrington docks, where steamers could c-ill and receive, or deliver goods, and the water was 26 feet deep, where boat could berth out of the fairway of the canal : also that line of rails was already laid on the sp3t in communica- tion with every large works in the town, and that Walton high road, adjacent, could be conveniently utilized forlocal traffic. This was pointed out to the !Directors before I retired from being Chairman of the Rivers Committee.

In conclusion, many years ago there used to be a free public wharf at B ink Quay where we have discharged grain, free of toll. We also bed for a considerable period, an engine on wheels, working upon a portion. of the land experimenting in the discharging of boats and cleaning the grain, and which was only removed at the time Messrs. Crosfield added our mill to enlarge their soap works. To my surprise, when last passing that way the wharf was boarded in, and " The Warrington Slate Company" was painted upon a board fastened on the door. Perhaps the Councillors mentioned by Messrs. Bishop and Co. as being returned for their .purpose, will esquire how this land has been acquired, and thereby restore once more a free whart for the public, as well as for Messrs. Bishop and 0 . —Yours faithfully, RICHARD T. FAIRCLOUGH.

Mersey Mills.

THE EXAMINER SATURDAY, DECEMBER 7, 1'395.

HALTO'N.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.--A. meeting of the Parish Council was held on Tuesday evening in the girls' schoolroom. The Rev. G. W. Wray occupied the chair, and there were also present, Messrs. Birch,J. S. Jeacock, R. Hughes, J. Looker, J. Stokes, J. Astbury,S. Harrow, and A. Woodier (clerk). The business of the meeting was with regard to the footpaths in this di •trict. The committee appointed to consider the matter, consisting of the chairman, Mr. J. Ackerley, and Aar. J. Looker, recommended several improvements and repairs which were adopted, and a committee consisting of the Chairman, Messrs. Ackerley, Looker, and Jeacock, was appointed with power to get the work done. It was thought that a number of local men out of work might be set to do some portions. The tender of Mr. Light- foot to improve the footpath running through his fields was accepted, and it was decided that he and other farmers be approached with regard to carting, etc. The Chairman announced that St. Thomas' Charity would be distributed on St. Thomas' Day, the 2lst inst., and he invited all the members who could possibly come, to be present at the distribution of the money.—This con- cluded the business.

Tait WESLEY GUILD. —On Monday evening, Mr. Walter Rowe read a paper on " The relation of Wesleyan Methodism to the Established Church." There were present 25 members, seven companions, and seven friends. Mr. S. Barrow presided. After the reading of the paper, there was a discussion in which the following took part : Mrs. Boys, Messrs. A. Cox, J. L.

persons Saunders and -S. Barrow.

rk WESLEYAN SALE OF WORK AT: PRESTON BROOK.

AN HISTORICAL SKETCH.

A successful two days' sale of work was opened in the Wesleyan Chapel, Preston Brook, on Wednesday after- noon. The object in view was the liquidating of a debt remaining on the chapel, and to raise funds for its reno- vation. The work offered for sale was the outcome of the Ladies' Guild, of wilich Mrs. Frodsham is prosident, Mrs. Massey secretary, and Mr. Frodsham the treasurer. There was a good display of useful and ornamental articles set out on six stalls in charge of the following : —Stall No. 1, Miss Jones and Miss M. Jones; 2, Miss Webb, Miss E. Davies, and Miss E. A. Clare ; 3, Miss M. Scragg, and Miss B. Webb ; 4, Miss W. Grounds, Miss E. Grounds ;5, Miss Scrag!, Miss M. Davies 8 (junior children), Misses Alice Ground, Alice Potts, May Rough. sedge, Carrie Johnson. There was, in addition, a re- freshment stall in charge of Miss R. Webb, Miss B. Webb, Miss F. Clare, and Miss M. Morris. There was a large attendance at the opening ceremony, many coming from the neighbouring district. Amongst those present were :—Rev. P.Brackenbury. Mr. John Simpson' Mr. and Mrs. J. R. Frith, and Mr. W. B. Frith, Messrs. James Johnson, H. Johnson, T. Johnson, T. Clarke, A. Jones, W. Hoxworth, Mrs. Earp,Mrs.Bee, Mrs. Grounds Mrs. McWhannell, Mrs. T. Webb, Mrs. Clare, Mrs. Withernshaw, Mrs. Rogers (Newton Bank), Miss Dutton (Moore), Mrs. Peter Scragg, Mrs. James Webb, and Mrs. Edward Scram The Rev. T. Brackenhury briefly introduced Mr. John Simpson, remarking that he took a keen interest in the spread of true Christianity throu4hout the locality. Mr. Simpson at the outset said he had taken an interest in the work at Preston Brook Cnape' for over 50 years. He was glad to see what had been going on, and he was pleased to see the place wonderfully improved in ap- pearance. Mr. Frodsham had been good enough to fur- nish him with some particulars about the Chapel in peat years, and he could add to these by personal knowledge. I He pointed out that the Chapel was opened on the 10th October, 1819, and that the services were conducted by the Rev. Robert Newton, and the Rev. Jonathan Crowther. The total cost of the ! chapel was £4BB and that of the school upwards of £ll9, and of the house £l9l, coming altogether wittdn a few pounds of £7OO. There had been a Wesleyan place of worship before that, at Preston-o'th•Hill, and Mr. Frodsham believed that the Primitive Methodist Chapel now stood on the old Wesleyan preaching place site. He (Mr. Simpson) used occasionally to go to Preston Brook, 50 or 60 years ago whets the mode of travelling was somewhat different than what it was to- day. When travelling from Runcorn. to Chester. .they went by boat to Preston Brook and thence proceeAd by omnibus to their destination. In 1848 the circuit *as divided but Preston Brook remained in the Warring- ton circuit. The Preston Brook friends. however. , thought they could be better served if transferred to the Runcorn circuit, and therefore it was arranged that they should be placed on the Runcorn plan. He had in his possession a piece of Riess from the win- dow of the old Wesleyan preaching place at Preston o'th Bill and on which the Rev. Samuel Bradburn had write 1 his name, and the motto " God is love." A sentat the back showed that the chapel was then )Liverpool circuit. ,Applause). ,e Rev. T. Brackenbury stated that 121 years ago Wesleyan Methodism had a cause at Preston o'th Hill. Wesleyan Methodism had been a blessing to tens of thou- sands in rural districts, and they did not mean to give up the villages. A vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Simpson, on the motion of Mr. Frodsham, seconded by Mr. Frith and supported by Mi. Johnson. • Wednesday's receipts amounted to £4O. The sale of work was again opened on Thursday, when Mr. F. J. Norman, J.P., of Runcorn, performed the opening ceremony.

1.0 G.T. EARLESTOWN EBENEZER LODGE.

This lodge held its usual weekly session on Tues- day evening. The lodge was called to order by Bro. W. Hatton. After the usual business was finished a very pleasant evening was spent with short readings, Bro. J. Jones being responsible. The lodge closed at the usual time.

BALL AT NEWTON.

Under the auspices of the Newton Cricket and Tennis club, a ball was given in the Town Hall, Newton, on Thursday night. There was a fair at- tendance and dancing was indulged in until an early hour on Friday morning to the strains of Mr. Peplow's string hand. THE Disradcr COUNCIL CLERKSHIP.

In response to the advertisement for a clerk to the Distiict Council 165 applications have been sent in. The sub-co amittee met on Wednesday evening to go through them, but owing to the number of applications frim really excellent men, they found their task a difficult one, and were unable to make a selection. About 50 have been selected, and these will be farther weeded on Monday.

CHRISTMAS ENDEAVOUR SOCIETY, NEWTON.

In connection with the junior branch of the Con- gregational Young People's Bociety of Christmas Endeavour, a sacred cantata entitled "Glad tidings," was given by a nnmber of the members of the Society, in the Congregational schoolroom, Newton on Wednesday evening. Owing to the rough state a the weather there was only a moderate atten- dance,but the children under the care of Miss Allan acquitted themselves remarkably well. M ARVELLOUS ESCAPE AT EARLESTOWN.

On Tuesday afternoon a driver in the employ of Mr. R. B. Toft, grocer, of Bridge-street, Earlestown, named William Jones, bad a narrow escape from serious injury. He was engaged loading parcels into a light spring cart opposite Mr. Toft's shop, when the horse suddenly swerved, Jones being thrown out. He fell with his head under the wheel, and before he could get oat of the way,the wheel passed over his bead. Fortunately however. he received no further injury than some slight bruises and con- tusions en each side of his face, and after being at- tended to was able to follow his employment.

ALLEGED THEFT OF A WATCH AT EARLBSTOWN. At the Earlestown Police Station on Monday morning before Mr. C. B. F. Borron,William Whittle was charged with stealing a silver lever watch and a silver guard, the property of his brother Isaac Whittle, of Earlestown-st.,Earlestown. The alleged theft was said to have been committed about 18 months ago from his father's houle,and the prisoner absconded. He was arrested on Monday, and re- manded to the Warrington Sessions. The prisoner was bronght up at the County Sessions, Warring- ton, on Wednesday morning, and was committed to gaol for 14 days. ENTERTAINMENT AT EARLESTOWN. GOOD TrktPLARS.

The weekly session of the Hope of Earlestown Lodge of Good Templars was held in the Co-opera- tive Hall, Earlestown, on Monday evening. Bro Hatton, C.T.,presided.and there was a large attend- lime of members of the lodge and officers af the No. 1 Convention were also present,including Bros. Roberts, Tomlin. and Dollin,of Warrington,Frances, of St. Helens, Taylor, of Earlestown, and others. Bro. Frances, of the Hope of Rsgby Lodge was also present. One candidate was initiated. Thanks were tendeted to Miss E. L. Greenall and others who assisted at the entertainment held on Saturday evening. Short addresses were given by Bros. Frances, Roberts, and Taylor, and thanks were ac- corded them for their visit. The lodge was then formally closed, and the programme of the evening an At Home," for which the Secretary was res- ponsible, was proceeded with, and the evening was spent in various games, refreshments being also handed round. SHOOTING MATCH AT NEWTON.

On Saturday afternoon a shooting match betwee mixed teams of the past and present members of the"o" Company of the South Lancashire Regiment P.W.volunteers stationed at Rarlestown, took place on the Highfield Moss range. The teams were under the captaincy of Colmar-Sergeant Dolan and Ser- geant Tarlatan, and although the weather was not favourable to high scoring, some good shooting was seen. In the end Colour-Sergeant Dolan's team won by 35 points. The stake was a tea to he paid for by the losing team, and after the match the teams sat down to a substantial repast at the Pied Bull Hotel provided by Mr. Dorman. At the con- clusion of the meal. Colour-Sergeant Haseldine took the chair, and Colour-Sergeant Dolan the vice- chair. Selections of music were given, and several toasts were honoured, amongst others being "The winning team." " The officers," " Highest scorers," and the reunion of the members of the Company was spoken of in very appreciative terms. One of the old members threw out a challenge to shoot "the past members versus present," which was readily accepted, and the match will be decided shortly.

DEATH OF Mit. W. BALL.

Sincere regret has --temen-expile;e-e-d--.at the prema- ture decease of Mr. William Ball, of Hawthorne ' Terrace, Crow-lane, Newton, at the age of 31 years. Mr. Ball has for about 16 years been employed in the office of the wagon works of the L. and N.-W. Railway at Earlestown, where he was much respected by his colleagues. He had also for about two years filled with ability the position of secre- tary of the Loyal Viaduct Lodge of Oddfellowa Til.U. He had been rather unwell since August last but it was not until a month ago that he had to relinquish his duties, and although he was assidu- ously attended by Dr. Valentine, he gradually grew worse, and finally succumbed on Friday morning , last, from chronic Bright's disease. He leaves a widow and one child. The interment took place on Tuesday at Wargrave Church. In addition to the relatives the deceased was followed to the grave by the whole of the office staff, and a large number of oadminws, including Mr. Thomas Lawton, r.p.G.AI,, West Derby Dian let , and Per- manent Secretary of the Loyal Travellers Rest Lodge, Gateacre and Woolston, near-Liver- pool. The coffin which was of polished oak with brass mountings was laden with numerous wreaths of choice flowers. The office staff sent a porcelain wreath with stand,and a porcelain wreath was sent by the Oddfellows Lodge. The funeral service was conducted by the Rev. E. W. Witten-. bury-Rave, and the whole arrangements were carried out by Mr. Stevens. i 0 - --- GRAVE CHARGE AGAINST A WARRINGTON YOUTH. On Monday morning at the Warrington Borough Police Court, before the Mayor (chairman), Alderman Harrison, Alderman Francomb, and Mr. T. Birchall, Samuel Williams, 19 years of age, ' residing at 28 Robson-st., and employe at Messrs. 'Viands, was charged -with committing a criminal assault on a girl named Lettice Warburton, aged 5 years and 10 montbs,and living at 35, Robson-street, on Satur- day afternoon..—The Chief Constable stated the case at some length to the bench, the facts being briefly these : On Saturday prisoner was standing at the door of his parents' house and the child and others were playing about the street. Prisoner called to her and asked her to bring him a packet of cigarettes from the shop of Mrs. Crutchley, in the same street. She did so and he took her into the house, and it is alleged committed the assault ho is charged with. Mr. Talbot asked that if,as he expected. he should make out a strong prima facia case, the prisoner be committed to the Assizes. The fol- lowing persons then gave evidence ;—Lettico Warburton (the child mentioned), Florence Crutchley (10), Amy Bannister (14), Mrs. Warburton, Mrs. Crutchley, Inspec- tor Spinks, Councillor Dr. Cannell and Dr. T. S. Smith. The two latter gentlemen stated that they had examined the child and they thought an assault had been com- mitted. . Prisoner was formally committed to the next Liver- pool ,Assize.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

UPROARIOUS SCENES. THE MEETING BROKEN UP IN CONFUSION.

The monthly meeting oh the -above Council was held at the Earlestown Town Hall on Monday afternoon, when the chair was occupied by Mr. C. B. F. Borron, J.P., C.C. There were also present Messrs. W. Beatty, J. Simpson. J. Smith, J.P., W. Gilmour, J. Slee, T. Ormand, W. Collingwood, W. Thompson, jun., P. Mac. Mahon, F. J. Wood, R. Stone, J.P., and E. T. Evans,and Rev. E. W. W. Kaye, and Mr. J. E. Worsley (law clerk).

MR. BRIERLEY'S ILLNESS.

The Chairman s-aic-1--jb-ai--tri-e-c;TiTcTi-Would be pleased to hear of a satisfactory improvement in the condition of Mr. Brierley. A letter had been received from him in which he regretted that by the advice of the doctor, he would not be able to attend the meeting that day. He had only been absent from one meeting for upwards of 20 years, and that was through illness. He theretore regretted that he must ask the C4uncil to excuse his ab- sence that day. The Chairman thought that Mr. Brier- ley's record was very satisfactory. (Hear, hear.)

THE; REA.DING OF THE COMMITTE.E'S MINUTES'.

Mr. MacMihon : What is the use of this notice asking us to discuss the minutes if you take them as read. The Chairman : That has been explained to you be- fore.

Mr. MacMahon : It has not, air. You cannot explain it.

The Chairman : The minutes have been printed and hpen placed in your hands two or three days ago, so as to give you ample time to read them fully. It is a mere waste of the time of the Council for the Clerk to read all the minutes which you have before you. Before the minutes were printed, it was necessary for the minutes to be read, bu^, it is not now necessary. It is the custom in other places.

Mr. MacMahon : It is a waste of ratepayers' money to pay for the notice being printed if the minutes are not discussed.

The Chairman : The motion does not deprive you of the opportunity of discussing every minute upon this paper,_

MacMahon,not being satisfied with this,exclaimed amid much disorder : You can confirm whatever you like, and as you think fit, but why do you have this notice printed and paid for if you don't act up to it. The Chairman : If Mr. MacMahon objects,or any gen- tleman insists upon the minutes being read, I must ask the members to submit to them being read. Mr. Gilmour hereupon rose and asked why it was that the report of a sub-committee which had been held the previous Monday, was not on the paper. He also alleged that this was an unauthorised committee meet- ing, but if it had been held, it should be put on the minutes.

Ths Chairman : Sub-committees report to the commit- tees.

Mr. Gilmour : But that was an unauthorised commit- tre,and decided some question last Monday. I was not asked to attend, and I want to know why the proceed- ings are not on the minutes. The Chairman : You are out of order.

Mr. Gilmour I am not oi.ii—o-f—O-iaer. (Cries of "Order.") The Chairman 1 I protest against your conduct, Mr. Gilmour. I cannot bear you any longer. Mr. Gilmour.: But the Council shall hear me (Great disorder and cries of "Chair," and "Sit down.") A great scene of confusion hereupon ensued, almost every member of the Council endeavouring to speak at once. During a temporary Jull,the Chairman asked Mr. MacMahon whether he still insisted upon having the printed minutes read, to which that gentleman replied, "ph, don't ask me. It's no use. Ido object to the minutes being taken as read. Mr. Gilmour (again rising) : I move that the com- mittee meeting be discussed, and that we know, before we proceed to anything else what was done at that com- mittee meeting. The Chairman : You are entirely out of order, and you must sit down. Mr. Gilmour : You must hear me, and I will not its down. (Loud cries of "Chair, chair." Mr. Thompson now got up to speak, but Mr. Gilmour I refused to sit down, and for a time both Mr. Thompson and Mr. Gilmour were talking together, and the noise and confusion were such as to render it almost impos- sible to know what was being said. The Chairman again told Mr.Gilmour to sit down,and called upon Mr. Thom- pson, but Mr. Gilmour shouting at the top of his voice, said that he would not sit down until his resolution had been carried. The Chairman again refused, and Mr. Thompson was understood to say that he seconded Mr. Collingwood's motion to take the minutes as read, but Mr. MacMahon here interrupted, and wanted to know which of the two gentlemen were to be listened to, and he proposed that the standing orders be suspended. The law clerk asked for the names of those who voted against the motion, but to this Mr.MacMahon replied : What's the use? You would not put them down correctly if they were given. (Loud uproar.) •

VOTE OF CENSURE ON MR. MACMAHON.

Mr. Stone said that he was surprised to hear a remark like that of Mr. MacMahon's from any member of the Council. He really thought that that called for a vote of censure. The Council should maintain their officials so as to enable them to command some respect. If such remarks had been necessary, they should have been made in committee and not in that public room. Mr. MacMahon : Many times my name has been put down as present at the committee meetings when I have been in the nest room.

Mr. Stone now formally moved a vote of censure on Mr.MacSlahon.

Mr. Gilmour opposed the resolution. Mr. Simpson seconded Mr. Stone's motion, and said that he thought it was simply disgraceful that such language should be used. - Mr. MacMahon got up to speak again, but the' chair- man promptly told him to sit down, to which he replied that he would not sit down for him. After further scenes, Mr. MacMahon said that he thought he was justified in his remarks as his name had been recorded wrongly on previous occasions. Mr. Evans : I think Mr. MacMahon might apologise. Mr. MacMahon : I will not do anything of the kind. Mr. Ormand said that he had no doubt that Mr. MacMahon was right when he said that his attendances were recorded incorrectly,but Mr. Worsley was certainly not to blame for that. He hoped that the resolution would not have to be put, as it made a precedent. He was sure that no member of the Council doubted the accuracy of Mr. Worsley's records but at the same time, he thought Mr. MacMahon had some cause for grievance although Mr. Worsley was not at fault. Mr. MacMahon still refused to withdraw his imputa- tion, although he denied saying that the law clerk had been the cause of the mistakes he complained of. The Chairman Said that he was sorry that Mr. Mac- Mahon bad not adopted the course suggested by Mr. Evans. It was a gentlemanly course to take, and it Mr. MacMahon had any cause to complain of any past meeting, he had had ample opportunity to have it cor- rected. As he had neglected to do so, it was his own fault that they had not been corrected. He must say with regard to Mr. MacMahon's conduct, that it was a disgrace, that language of that sort was used to the officials of that Council, not only on that occasion but lon many previous occasions. (Cries of "hear, hear.") 1 Mr. MacMahon: Leave other times alone. You are censuring me for to-day. The Chairman: I think it is lowering to the dignity of the Council that the officials should be spoken about in that fashion by members of tbe Council, as they were not in the same position to defend themselves. He thought that too much insulting language had been used to the officials, and lie now put the resolution of Mr. Stone to the meeting. Mr. MacMahon: Allow me to speak. The Chairman: We have allowed you to Speak too much. If you are going to apologise, I shall allow you to speak. Mr. MacMahon: Name the words I must say. Mr. Stone here said that he-had taken down the words which had been said, and ho read them: Turning to Mr. MacMahon, he asked him whether he would ; apologise for those words. If he would, he would have great pleasure in withdrawing his resolution. Mr. MacMahon: I will not do it. The resolution was then put, and only Messts. Mac- Mahon and Gilmour voted against it. Mr. Gilmour again endeavoured to speak but the Chairman refused to allaw him, and he then said that there would be plenty of opportunity yet for speaking. THE BREA% UP ICIF. THE MEETING. Mr. Ormand said that as that meeting bad reference to some fraud or appropriation of the Council's money, he thought certainly that he, as chairman of the Finance Committee ought to have been invited to attend the meeting. - SALE OF WORK AT EARLESTOWS.

SPEECH BY THE HON. T. W. LEGIT, M.P.

On Saturday afternoon the Hon. T. W.Legh, M.P., opened a Chinese fair and sale of work in the St. John's Parochial Hall, Earlestown, in connection with St. John's Church. The sale of work was in aid of a debt of about £65 &ill remaining on the hall, which it was anticipated would be entirely cleared off by this effort. The room had been very nicely laid out, a number of stalls laden with a variety of articles of a useful and ornamental character, having been erected down each aide, whilst the side shows comprised a galvanic battery, kicking the football, quoits, living pictures, wax- works, and art gallery, &c. There was also a Christmas tree with numerous fancy articles sus- pended from its branches, and a bran pie in which the visitors dipped to try their luck. At the open- ing there were present, the Revs. T. R. Mathews, E. W. Wittenbury-Kay, Mr. C. B. F. Horton, Mr. T. Pimblett, Mr. J. Rylance and a large number of the general public. The Rev. T. R. Matthews, by way of introduction,said they were all aware of the object, the promotion of which they were met together for that afternoon. They all knew that some time ago they had a very great need for such a room as that, and a good many of them saw the building and completion, and also witnessed the opening. Many of them had also used the room since for many purposes. Now they were met t,gether to try and wipe off the small debt which remained, and their ' friend, Mr. Legh, their worthy member wbo was always ready to help, had very kindly come a long way that day for the purpose of opening that sale of work. (Ap- plause.) Mr. T. A. Pimblett (churchwarden) wished the bazaar every success. He thought it was the third bazaar they had held. They had one in the year 1890 at which they did very well, and which Mr. and Mrs. Legh very kindly came and opened for them. The second was held in 1893, and they did very well at that,but they wanted to do better on this occasion, and to clear the small debt off. He thought the debt was about £6O or £7O and they ought to clear that off very easily that day. (Hear, hear.) The Hon. T. W. Legh, M.P., said the object for which they were assembled that afternoon, and in support of which they had done him the honour of inviting him to be present, was one which required very few words of recommendation from himself. He had always been told, and he was entirely of the same opinion himself, that upon similar occasions, the least said the better. People were assembled there for the purpose of business, and those were not the opportunities which ought to be taken advantage of for delivering lengthy speeches. That building he believed had been in existence for three years, and he thought he officiated at its birth. For those three years it had served many purposes, and had also been a centre of useful and bene- ficial work. (Hear, hear.) In the language of the wild- est flights of imagination one could not describe the building as architecturally beautiful,but it was a monu- ment of the zeal and activity of the members of a particu- lar congregation. There was a common delusion, which was shared by many ignorant people, that everybody in Lancashire,and every place in Lancashire was so abnor- mally prosperous that there was no difficulty whatever in finding money.lle only wished that delusion was true. That particular parish was at once the largest and smallest of the three which were contained in the town- ship of Makerfield, and perhaps it was owing to this that this particular Mission Hall had not received any supportifrom the various churches and missionary socie- ties in the neighbourhood. It must be borne in mind that Earlestown was composed of the working class, and wealthy residents were conspicious by their absence. That building had cost something like, £B7O of that sum no leas than £5OO had been contributed by members of the congregation, who were, he believed, all members of the working class also, and it was highly to the credit of those persons in spite of contributions from outside, and five persons who did not reside in the town, there was only something like a debt of £65 to clear off in order to put the establishment on a sound financial basis. He did not think there would be much difficulty In obtaining that amount !which he might des- cribe as a small sum. If he might judge from the large number of persons he saw before him, he was sure a much larger sum would be realised, and he was sure those people who had given their time, and service in making the exhibition, would meet with their due re- ward. He then formally declared the sale open. Mr. Pimblett moved a hearty v Ate of thanks to Mr. Leah. Mr. J. Itylance seconded and it was heartily ac- corded. The Rev. 1". R. Matthews said their member had spokon or the plainness of their structure. The late Canon Whitley, some 18 years ago, told him that they wanted a useful and not an ornamental parson for the pariah. (Laughter.) The sale was then briskly nro- ceeded with, the takings for the day being about £53. The sale was continued on Monday, when the receipts amounted to about £32, the total for the two days be- ing upwards of £B5. A large number of articles remain unsold, which will be disposed of by public auction later on.

The Rev. W. Arrowsmith in the course of hist addrefil, referred to the tact and energy and self sacrifice which his colleagues had manifested,anc he could only hope that the result of that bazaar might be the consummation Of 'their highest hopes. He believed that dune" the time • of the bazaar, they would be favoured with the Byre- pathy of the circuit at least, and he hoped with the - Presence of a good many people from different parts of the circuit. They must net forget that they had had an overplus of bazaars in the neighbourhood, and some of their friends were almost afraid of the name of a bazaas's a?d if they were not present, it was riot because they did not sympathise with them, and wish them well, but perhaps other reasons prevented them being present. The presence of Mrs. Walker that afternoon showed that they appreciated the services she and her husband had rendered in days gone by. (Applause.) Mrs. Walker said it was a great pleasure to her to be present that afternoon, a pleasure which was greatly' increased because of meeting with old friends and fellow - workers, although she missed many of the old faces, the memory of which was very dear to her. They had been promoted, and now rest from their labours, but. the effects of their lives were seen there that day. The little seed scattered here and there bad brought forth fruit. When she looked round at the - stalls, and thought of the amount of labour,she tharikec, God on their behalf. They had always been a self hell?: society, and the words of St. 1-aul to the Romans be applicable to them " Be patient and weary rot In well doing." She was often busy at 'bazaar work, and that was the sixth she had had to do with recently. bt:l . felt thankful that women took such an active part in the work of God ; she liked to give women a ward encouragement. What would their beloved alethooltsPa be to-day without women? She failed to see the evil ar? b alars if conducted aright, that some people .speak about. Did they not teach our young people thrift awe_ industry, and were they not better fitted to becol.3. wives and mothers? Princess May set them all a splendis:. example ; if a friend called to see her she did not put he work down, but made an apology by saying allelic" r besy, ths. w ould excuse her going on with her V7ol* . i 1. S = She r eluded by declariug the bazaar open, and hope u t would he a fin +ncial success. (Applause.) The Rev. J. Bartley moved a vote of than to Walker, and and the Chairman, which Mr. P. Parr seconded, and it was heartily accorded. Many of those present then adjourned to the luncheon__ table were a most substantial repast was served.. In! sale was afterwards proceeded with, and until o'clock it was pursued with much vigour. The varied! side shows did very good business, especially the" Viral work" in charge of Prof. Clairiouni and his man There - There is no doubt the " Waxwork " was well worth at visit, because of the manner in which the affair IV?* managed. WESLEYAN BAZAAR AT EARLESTOWN.

A three days'hazaar in connection with the Earleqtown I Wesleyan Church, was opened in the Town Hall, Earles- I town, on Thursday, by Mrs. B. Walker, of Southport. The members of the congregation have for months past been busily engaged preparing for thia bazaar, their object being to raise about 1.'400, for the purpose of extinguishing the debt remaining on the Trust premises, to provide funds for improved ventilation, and for refurnishing. No effort has been spared to ensure success, and for this purpose the services of Messrs. Cowgill and Co., of Blackburn, were secured to arrange the large room of the Town Hall to represent as early English or 17th century Market Place. Taking the de- I scriptinn supplied bylthe scenic artigtes, we might say that on the platform was placed a large gateway, such as might have been seen in former times, denoting the entrance to a walled city, and somewhat resembling that relic of by-gone days so recently removed in London, viz., Temple Bar, and this forms a striking feature of the decorations. In front of the walls and arranged on each side of the room are quaint old houses and shops of the period, with their latticed windows, high pointed gabled roofs, the fronts showing the old oak beams and sup- ports so largely used in those primitive days, and which form such a distinctive feature of the architecture of the period, with here and there the old time-worn projecting signs. These are built in model, with the stalls under- neath, forming as it were, shops tilled with a liberal display of all kinds of useful and ornamental articles for sale. At the opposite end of the room the gallery is treated harmoniously with the rest of the design ; the front of it so arranged as to form a wall with the scenery placed a short distance behind it, so that the visitor may ascend and look over the battlements at the busy scene below. In the centre of the room is an old English Market Close, at the base of which are disposed flowers and plants. There were altogether seven stalls, including the Ladies' Sewing Meeting Stall (2), Young Ladies' Fancy Stall, Young Men's Stall, Sunday School Children's Stall,Flower Stall, and Refreshment Stall—all of which were presided over by a large staff of ladies and gentlemen. The whole arrangements were carried out by a numerous committee, of which the Rev. John Bart- ley was president. hon. treasurer, Mr. George Burgess ; hon. sec., Mr. P. Parr ; hon. assist. sec., Mr. S. Barrett. The gathering on Thursday was presided over by the Rev. W. Arrowsmith,wha was accompanied on the plat- form by Mrs. Walker, Rev.J. T. Waddy,B.A., S. Harper E. R. Davies, and Mr. P. Parr (sea.). Amongst those. Present in the body of the hall were :—Mesers. J. W. Emmett, E. T. Evans. C. W. Emmett, James Howard (Seaforth), G. Owen, P. Chandley, J.Roherts, T Sander. igen, J. Gooding, A. Cawley, and Rev. H. S. Sandford. After the singing of a hymn 'prayer was offered byithe Rev. J. T. Waddy, 8.A., of Pruett.
